# Marginal likelihood computation for model selection and hypothesis testing: an extensive review

F. Llorente*, L. Martino*, D. Delgado*, J. Lopez-Santiago*

$^{\star}$  Universidad Carlos III de Madrid, Leganés (Spain).

$\star \star$  Universidad Rey Juan Carlos, Fuenlabrada (Spain).

# Abstract

This is an up-to-date introduction to, and overview of, marginal likelihood computation for model selection and hypothesis testing. Computing normalizing constants of probability models (or ratio of constants) is a fundamental issue in many applications in statistics, applied mathematics, signal processing and machine learning. This article provides a comprehensive study of the state-of-the-art of the topic. We highlight limitations, benefits, connections and differences among the different techniques. Problems and possible solutions with the use of improper priors are also described. Some of the most relevant methodologies are compared through theoretical comparisons and numerical experiments.

Keywords: Marginal likelihood, Bayesian evidence, numerical integration, model selection, hypothesis testing, quadrature rules, double-intractable posteriors, partition functions

# 1 Introduction

Marginal likelihood (a.k.a., Bayesian evidence) and Bayes factors are the core of the Bayesian theory for testing hypotheses and model selection [1, 2]. More generally, the computation of normalizing constants or ratios of normalizing constants has played an important role in statistical physics and numerical analysis [3]. In the Bayesian setting, the approximation of normalizing constants is also required in the study of the so-called double intractable posteriors [4].

Several methods have been proposed for approximating the marginal likelihood and normalizing constants in the last decades. Most of these techniques have been originally introduced in the field of statistical mechanics. Indeed, the marginal likelihood is the analogous of a central quantity in statistical physics known as the partition function which is also closely related to another important quantity often called free-energy. The relationship between statistical physics and Bayesian inference has been remarked in different works [5, 6].

The model selection problem has been also addressed from different points of view. Several criteria have been proposed to deal with the trade-off between the goodness-of-fit of the model and its simplicity. For instance, the Akaike information criterion (AIC) or the focused information criterion (FIC) are two examples of these approaches [7, 8]. The Bayesian-Schwarz information

criterion (BIC) is related to the marginal likelihood approximation, as discussed in Section 3. The deviance information criterion (DIC) is a generalization of the AIC, which is often used in Bayesian inference [9, 10]. It is particularly useful for hierarchical models and it can be approximately computed when the outputs of a Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) algorithm are given. However, DIC is not directly related to the Bayesian evidence [11]. Another different approach, also based on information theory, is the so-called minimum description length principle (MDL) [12]. MDL was originally derived for data compression, and then was applied to model selection and hypothesis testing. Roughly speaking, MDL considers that the best explanation for a given set of data is provided by the shortest description of that data [12].

In the Bayesian framework, there are two main classes of sampling algorithms. The first one consists in approximating the marginal likelihood of different models or the ratio of two marginal likelihoods. In this work, we focus on this first approach. The second sampling approach extends the posterior space including a discrete indicator variable  $m$ , denoting the  $m$ -th model [13, 14]. For instance, in the well-known reversible jump MCMC [14], a Markov chain is generated in this extended space, allowing jumps between models with possibly different dimensions. However, generally, these methods are difficult to tune and the mixing of the chain can be poor [15]. For further details, see also the interesting works [16, 17, 18]. The average number of MCMC iterations when the chain jumps or stays into the  $m$ -th model is proportional to the marginal likelihood of the corresponding model.

In this work, we provide an extensive review of computational techniques for the marginal likelihood computation. The main contribution is to present jointly numerous computational schemes (introduced independently in the literature) with a detailed description under the same notation, highlighting their differences, relationships, limitations and strengths. Most of them are based on the importance sampling (IS) approach and several of them are combination the MCMC and IS schemes. It is also important to remark that parts of the presented material are also novel, i.e., no contained in previous works. We have widely studied, analyzed and jointly described with a unique notation and classification, the methodologies presented in a vast literature from 1990s to the recent proposed algorithms (see Table 24). We also discuss issues and solutions when improper priors are employed. Therefore, this survey provides an ample covering of the literature, where we highlight important details and comparisons in order to facilitate the understanding of the interested readers and practitioners.

The problem statement and the main notation are introduced in the Section 2.1. Relevant considerations regarding the marginal likelihood and other model selection strategies are given in Section 2.2 and Section 7. Specifically, a description of how the marginal likelihood handles the model fit and the model complexity is provided in Section 2.2. The dependence on the prior selection and the possible choice of an improper prior are discussed in Section 7. The different techniques have been classified in four main families, as shown in Section 2.3. Sections 3, 4, 5, 6 are devoted to the detailed description of the computational schemes for approximating the Bayesian evidence. Section 8 contains some numerical experiments. In Section 9, we conclude with a final summary and discussion. We provide also theoretical analyses of some of the experiments and other comparisons in the Supplementary Material.

# 2 Problem statement and preliminary discussions

# 2.1 Framework and notation

In many applications, the goal is to make inference about a variable of interest,  $\pmb{\theta} = \theta_{1:D_{\theta}} = [\theta_1,\theta_2,\dots,\theta_{D_\theta}]\in \Theta \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{D_\theta}$ , where  $\theta_d\in \mathbb{R}$  for all  $d = 1,\ldots ,D_{\theta}$ , given a set of observed measurements,  $\mathbf{y} = [y_{1},\dots,y_{D_{y}}]\in \mathbb{R}^{D_{y}}$ . In the Bayesian framework, one complete model  $\mathcal{M}$  is formed by a likelihood function  $\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta},\mathcal{M})$  and a prior probability density function (pdf)  $g(\pmb {\theta}|\mathcal{M})$ . All the statistical information is summarized by the posterior pdf, i.e.,

$$
P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \mathcal {M}) = \frac {\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} , \mathcal {M}) g (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathcal {M})}{p (\mathbf {y} | \mathcal {M})}, \tag {1}
$$

where

$$
Z = p (\mathbf {y} \mid \mathcal {M}) = \int_ {\Theta} \ell (\mathbf {y} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta}, \mathcal {M}) g (\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \mathcal {M}) d \boldsymbol {\theta}, \tag {2}
$$

is the so-called marginal likelihood, a.k.a., Bayesian evidence. This quantity is important for model selection purpose, as we show below. However, usually  $Z = p(\mathbf{y}|\mathcal{M})$  is unknown and difficult to approximate, so that in many cases we are only able to evaluate the unnormalized target function,

$$
\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \mathcal {M}) = \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}, \mathcal {M}) g (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathcal {M}). \tag {3}
$$

Note that  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\mathcal{M}) \propto \pi (\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\mathcal{M})$  [1, 2]. For the sake of simplicity, hereafter we use the simplified notation  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  and  $\pi (\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . Thus, note that

$$
Z = \int_ {\Theta} \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) d \boldsymbol {\theta}. \tag {4}
$$

Model Selection and testing hypotheses. Let us consider now  $M$  possible models (or hypotheses),  $\mathcal{M}_1, \ldots, \mathcal{M}_M$ , with prior probability mass  $p_m = \mathbb{P}(\mathcal{M}_m)$ ,  $m = 1, \ldots, M$ . Note that, we can have variables of interest  $\pmb{\theta}^{(m)} = [\theta_1^{(m)}, \theta_2^{(m)}, \ldots, \theta_{D_m}^{(m)}] \in \Theta_m \in \mathbb{R}^{D_m}$ , with possibly different dimensions in the different models. The posterior of the  $m$ -th model is given by

$$
p (\mathcal {M} _ {m} | \mathbf {y}) = \frac {p _ {m} p (\mathbf {y} | \mathcal {M} _ {m})}{p (\mathbf {y})} \propto p _ {m} Z _ {m} \tag {5}
$$

where  $Z_{m} = p(\mathbf{y}|\mathcal{M}_{m}) = \int_{\Theta}\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}_{m},\mathcal{M}_{m})g(\pmb{\theta}_{m}|\mathcal{M}_{m})d\pmb{\theta}_{m}$ , and  $p(\mathbf{y}) = \sum_{m=1}^{M}p(\mathcal{M}_{m})p(\mathbf{y}|\mathcal{M}_{m})$ . Moreover, the ratio of two marginal likelihoods

$$
\frac {Z _ {m}}{Z _ {m ^ {\prime}}} = \frac {p (\mathbf {y} | \mathcal {M} _ {m})}{p (\mathbf {y} | \mathcal {M} _ {m ^ {\prime}})} = \frac {p (\mathcal {M} _ {m} | \mathbf {y}) / p _ {m}}{p (\mathcal {M} _ {m ^ {\prime}} | \mathbf {y}) / p _ {m ^ {\prime}}}, \tag {6}
$$

also known as Bayes factors, represents the posterior to prior odds of models  $m$  and  $m'$ . If some quantity of interest is common to all models, the posterior of this quantity can be studied via model averaging [19], i.e., a complete posterior distribution as a mixture of  $M$  partial posteriors linearly combined with weights proportionally to  $p(\mathcal{M}_m|\mathbf{y})$  (see, e.g., [20, 21]). Therefore, in all these scenarios, we need the computation of  $Z_m$  for all  $m = 1,\dots,M$ . In this work, we describe different computational techniques for calculating  $Z_m$ , mostly based on Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) and Importance Sampling (IS) algorithms [2]. Hereafter, we assume proper prior  $g(\pmb{\theta}|\mathcal{M}_m)$ . Regarding the use of improper priors see Section 7.2. Moreover, we usually denote  $Z$ ,  $\Theta$ ,  $\mathcal{M}$ , omitting the subindex  $m$ , to simplify notation. It is important also to remark that, in some cases, it is also necessary to approximate normalizing constants (that are also functions of the parameters) in each iteration of an MCMC algorithm, in order to allow the study of the posterior density. For instance, this is the case of the so-called double intractable posteriors [4].

Remark 1. The evidence  $Z$  is the normalizing constant of  $\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ , hence most of the methods in this review can be used to approximate normalizing constants of generic pdfs.

Remark 2. Instead of approximating the single values  $Z_{m}$  for all  $m$ , another approach consists in estimating directly the ratio of two marginal likelihoods  $\frac{Z_{m}}{Z_{m^{\prime}}}$ , i.e., approximating directly the Bayes factors. For these reasons, several computational methods focus on estimating the ratio of two normalizing constants. However, they can be used also for estimating a single  $Z_{m}$  provided that  $Z_{m^{\prime}}$  is known.

# 2.2 Model fit and model complexity

# 2.2.1 Bounds of the evidence  $Z$

Let us denote the maximum and minimum value of the likelihood function as  $\ell_{\mathrm{min}} = \ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{min}}) = \min_{\pmb{\theta}\in \pmb{\theta}}\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta})$  , and  $\ell_{\mathrm{max}} = \ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{max}}) = \max_{\pmb{\theta}\in \pmb{\theta}}\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta})$  , respectively. Note that

$$
Z = \int_ {\Theta} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \pmb {\theta}) g (\pmb {\theta}) d \pmb {\theta} \leq \ell (\mathbf {y} | \pmb {\theta} _ {\mathrm {m a x}}) \int_ {\Theta} g (\pmb {\theta}) d \pmb {\theta} = \ell (\mathbf {y} | \pmb {\theta} _ {\mathrm {m a x}}).
$$

Similarly, we can obtain  $Z \geq \ell(\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{min}})$ . The maximum and minimum value of  $Z$  are reached with two degenerate choices of the prior,  $g(\pmb{\theta}) = \delta (\pmb{\theta} - \pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{max}})$  and  $g(\pmb{\theta}) = \delta (\pmb{\theta} - \pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{min}})$ . Hence, for every other choice of  $g(\pmb{\theta})$ , we have

$$
\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\min }) \leq Z \leq \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\max }) \tag {7}
$$

Namely, depending on the choice of the prior  $g(\pmb{\theta})$ , we can have any value of Bayesian evidence contained in the interval  $[ \ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{min}}), \ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{max}}) ]$ . For further discussion see Section 7.

The two possible extreme values correspond to the worst and the best model fit, respectively. Below, we will see that if  $Z = \ell(\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{min}})$  the chosen prior,  $g(\pmb{\theta}) = \delta(\pmb{\theta} - \pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{min}})$ , applies the greatest possible penalty to the model whereas, if  $Z = \ell(\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{max}})$ , the chosen prior,  $g(\pmb{\theta}) = \delta(\pmb{\theta} - \pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{max}})$ , does not apply any penalization to the model complexity (we have the maximum overfitting). Namely, the evidence  $Z$  is an average of the likelihood values, weighted according to the prior.

# 2.2.2 Occam factor and implicit/intrinsic complexity penalization in  $Z$

The marginal likelihood can be expressed as

$$
Z = \ell_ {\mathrm {m a x}} W, \tag {8}
$$

where  $W \in [0,1]$  is the Occam factor [22, Sect. 3]. More specifically, the Occam factor is defined as

$$
W = \frac {1}{\ell_ {\operatorname* {m a x}}} \int_ {\Theta} g (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) d \boldsymbol {\theta}, \tag {9}
$$

and it is  $\frac{\ell_{\mathrm{min}}}{\ell_{\mathrm{max}}} \leq W \leq 1$ . The factor  $W$  measures the penalty of the model complexity intrinsically contained in the marginal likelihood  $Z$ : this penalization depends on the chosen prior and the number of data involved. We show below that the Occam factor measures the "overlap" between likelihood and prior, i.e., how diffuse the prior is with respect to the likelihood function. Finally, it is important to remark that, considering the posterior of the  $m$ -th model  $p(\mathcal{M}_m|\mathbf{y})$ , we have another possible penalization term due to the prior  $p_m = P(\mathcal{M}_m) \in [0,1]$ , i.e.,

$$
p (\mathcal {M} _ {m} | \mathbf {y}) \propto Z p _ {m} = \ell_ {\mathrm {m a x}} W p _ {m} = \ell_ {\mathrm {m a x}} \widetilde {W},
$$

where we have defined the posterior Occam factor as  $\widetilde{W} = Wp_{m}$

# 2.2.3 Occam factor with uniform priors

One-dimensional case. Let start with a single parameter,  $\theta = \theta$ , and a uniform prior in  $[a,b]$ . We can define the amount of the likelihood mass is contained inside the prior bounds,

$$
\Delta_ {\ell} = \frac {1}{\ell_ {\operatorname* {m a x}}} \int_ {a} ^ {b} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \theta) d \theta , \quad \text {w h e r e} \quad \ell_ {\max } = \max  _ {\theta \in \theta} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \theta). \tag {10}
$$

Defining also the width of the prior as  $\Delta_{\theta} = |\Theta| = b - a$ , note that  $0 \leq \Delta_{\ell} \leq \Delta_{\theta}$ , where the equality  $\Delta_{\ell} = \Delta_{\theta}$  is given when the likelihood is  $\ell(\mathbf{y}|\theta) = \ell_{\max}$  is constant. The Occam factor is given as the ratio of  $\Delta_{\ell}$  and the width of a uniform prior  $\Delta_{\theta}$  [22],

$$
W = \frac {\Delta_ {\ell}}{\Delta_ {\theta}}. \tag {11}
$$

If the likelihood function is integrable in  $\mathbb{R}$ , then there exists a finite upper bound for  $\Delta_{\ell}$  when  $\Delta_{\theta} \to \infty$ , that is  $\Delta_{\ell}^{*} = \frac{1}{\ell_{\mathrm{max}}} \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty} \ell(\mathbf{y}|\theta)d\theta$ . Hence, in this scenario, we can see that an increase of  $\Delta_{\theta}$  makes that  $W$  approaches 0.

Multidimensional case. Consider now a multidimensional case,  $\pmb{\theta} = [\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\dots,\theta_{D_{\theta}}]\in \Theta \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{D_{\theta}}$  where we can use the same uniform prior, with the same width  $\Delta_{\theta} = |\Theta|$ , for all the parameters. In this case,  $\Delta_{\ell} = \frac{1}{\ell_{\mathrm{max}}}\int_{\Theta}\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta})d\pmb {\theta}\leq (\Delta_{\theta})^{D_{\theta}}$  is  $D_{\theta}$ -dimensional integral, and  $\ell_{\mathrm{max}} = \max \ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta})$ . Then, for  $D_{\theta}$  parameters, the Occam factor is

$$
W = \frac {\Delta_ {\ell}}{(\Delta_ {\theta}) ^ {D _ {\theta}}}. \tag {12}
$$

Usually, as  $D_{\theta}$  grows, the fitting improves until reaching (or approaching) a maximum, possible overfitting. Then, with  $D_{\theta}$  big enough,  $\ell_{\mathrm{max}}$  tends to be virtually constant (reaching the maximum overfitting). If  $\int_{\Theta} \ell(\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}) d\pmb{\theta}$  grows slower than  $(\Delta_{\theta})^{D_{\theta}}$  as  $D_{\theta} \to \infty$ , and assuming for an illustrative purpose  $\Delta_{\theta} > 1$ , then  $W$  converges to 0 as  $D_{\theta} \to \infty$ . That is, when we introduce more and more parameters, the increase in model fit will be dominated, at some point, by the model complexity penalization implicitly contained in the evidence  $Z$ .

# 2.2.4 Marginal likelihood and information criteria

Considering the expressions (8) and (12) and taking the logarithm, we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \log Z = \log \ell_ {\max } + \log W = \log \ell_ {\max } + \log \Delta_ {\ell} - D _ {\theta} \log \Delta_ {\theta}, \\ = \log \ell_ {\max } + \eta D _ {\theta}, \tag {13} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\eta = \frac{\log\Delta_{\ell}}{D_{\theta}} - \log \Delta_{\theta}$  is a constant value, which also depends on the number of data  $D_y$  and, generally,  $\eta = \eta (D_y,D_\theta)$ . Different model selection rules in the literature consider the simplification  $\eta = \eta (D_y)$ . Note that  $\log \ell_{\mathrm{max}}$  is a fitting term whereas  $\eta D_{\theta}$  is a penalty for the model complexity. Instead of maximizing  $Z$  (or  $\log Z$ ) for model selection purposes, several authors consider the minimization of some cost functions derived by different information

criteria. To connect them with the marginal likelihood maximization, we consider the expression of  $-2\log Z = -2I$  where  $I = -\log Z$  resembles the Shannon information associated to  $Z = p(\mathbf{y})$ , i.e.,

$$
2 I = - 2 \log Z = - 2 \log \ell_ {\max } - 2 \eta D _ {\theta}. \tag {14}
$$

The expression above encompasses several well-known information criteria proposed in the literature and shown in Table 2, which differ for the choice of  $\eta$ . In all these cases,  $\eta$  is just a function of the number of data  $D_y$ . More details regarding these information criteria are given in Section 3.

Remark 3. The penalty term in the information criteria is the same for every parameter. The Bayesian approach allows the choice of different penalties, assuming different priors, one for each parameter.

# 2.3 A general overview of the computational methods

After a depth revision of the literature, we have recognized four main families of techniques, described below. We list them in order of complexity, from the simplest to the most complex underlying main idea. However, each class can contain both simple and very sophisticated algorithms.

Family 1: Deterministic approximations. These methods consider an analytical approximation of the function  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . The Laplace method and the Bayesian Information Criterion (BIC), belongs to this family (see Section 3).

Family 2: Methods based on density estimation. This class of algorithms uses the equality

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\widehat {P} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}, \tag {15}
$$

where  $\widehat{P}(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{y}) \approx P(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{y})$  represents an estimation of the density  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  at some point  $\pmb{\theta}^{*}$ . Generally, the point  $\pmb{\theta}^{*}$  is chosen in a high-probability region. The techniques in this family differ in the procedure employed for obtaining the estimation  $\widehat{P}(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{y})$ . One famous example is the Chib's method [25]. Section 3 is devoted to describe methods belonging to family 1 and family 2.

Family 3: Importance sampling (IS) schemes. The IS methods are based on rewriting Eq. (2) as an expected value w.r.t. a simpler normalized density  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$ , i.e.,  $Z = \int_{\Theta} \pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}) d\pmb{\theta} = E_{\bar{q}}\left[\frac{\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})}{\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})}\right]$ . This is the most considered class of methods in the literature, containing numerous variants, extensions and generalizations. We devote Sections 4-5 to this family of techniques.

Family 4: Methods based on a vertical representation. These schemes rely on changing the expression of  $Z = \int_{\Theta} \ell(\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}) g(\pmb{\theta}) d\pmb{\theta}$  (that is a multidimensional integral) to equivalent one-dimensional integrals [26, 27, 28]. Then, a quadrature scheme is applied to approximate this one-dimensional integral. The most famous example is the nested sampling algorithm [28]. Section 6 is devoted to this class of methods.

# 3 Methods based on deterministic approximations and density estimation

In this section, we consider approximations of  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ , or its unnormalized version  $\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ , in order to obtain an estimation  $Z$ . In a first approach, the methods consider  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  or  $\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  as a function, and try to obtain a good approximation given another parametric or non-parametric family of functions. Another approach consists in approximating  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  only at one specific point  $\pmb{\theta}^*$ , i.e.,  $\widehat{P}(\pmb{\theta}^*|\mathbf{y}) \approx P(\pmb{\theta}^*|\mathbf{y})$  ( $\pmb{\theta}^*$  is usually chosen in high posterior probability regions), and then using the identity

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\widehat {P} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}. \tag {16}
$$

The latter scheme is often called candidate's estimation.

# 3.1 Laplace's method

Let us define  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}_{\mathrm{MAP}} \approx \pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{MAP}} = \arg \max P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  (obtained by some optimization method), which is an approximation of the maximum a posteriori (MAP), and consider a Gaussian approximation of  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  around  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}_{\mathrm{MAP}}$ , i.e.,

$$
\widehat {P} (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) = \mathcal {N} (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {M A P}}, \widehat {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}}), \tag {17}
$$

with  $\widehat{\Sigma} \approx -\mathbf{H}^{-1}$ , which is an approximation of the negative inverse Hessian matrix of  $\log \pi(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  at  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}_{\mathrm{MAP}}$ . Replacing in Eq. (16), with  $\pmb{\theta}^{*} = \widehat{\pmb{\theta}}_{\mathrm{MAP}}$ , we obtain the Laplace approximation

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {\pi (\widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {M A P}} | \mathbf {y})}{\mathcal {N} (\widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {M A P}} | \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {M A P}}, \widehat {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}})} = (2 \pi) ^ {\frac {D _ {x}}{2}} | \widehat {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}} | ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \pi (\widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {M A P} | \mathbf {y}}). \tag {18}
$$

This is equivalent to the classical derivation of Laplace's estimator, which is based on expanding the log  $\pi (\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y}) = \log (\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta})g(\pmb {\theta}))$  as quadratic around  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}_{\mathrm{MAP}}$  and substituting in  $Z = \int \pi (\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y})d\pmb{\theta}$

that is,

$$
\begin{array}{l} Z = \int \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} = \int \exp \left\{\log \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) \right\} d \boldsymbol {\theta} (19) \\ \approx \int \exp \left\{\log \pi \left(\widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {M A P}} | \mathbf {y}\right) - \frac {1}{2} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} - \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {M A P}}\right) ^ {T} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}} ^ {- 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} - \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {M A P}}\right) \right\} d \boldsymbol {\theta} (20) \\ = (2 \pi) ^ {\frac {D _ {x}}{2}} | \widehat {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}} | ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \pi (\widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {M A P}} | \mathbf {y}). (21) \\ \end{array}
$$

In [29], they propose to use samples generated by a Metropolis-Hastings algorithm to estimate the quantities  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}_{\mathrm{MAP}}$  and  $\widehat{\Sigma}$  [2]. The resulting method is called Laplace-Metropolis estimator. The authors in [30] present different variants of the Laplace's estimator. A relevant extension for Gaussian Markov random field models, is the so-called integrated nested Laplace approximation (INLA) [31].

# 3.2 Bayesian-Schwarz information criterion (BIC)

Let us define  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}_{\mathrm{MLE}}\approx \pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{MLE}} = \arg \max \ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta})$  . The following quantity

$$
\operatorname {B I C} = D _ {\theta} \log D _ {y} - 2 \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {M L E}}), \tag {22}
$$

was introduced by Gideon E. Schwarz in [23], where  $D_{\theta}$  represents the number of parameters of the model  $(\pmb {\theta}\in \mathbb{R}^{D_{\theta}})$ ,  $D_{y}$  is the number of data, and  $\ell (\mathbf{y}|\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}_{\mathrm{MLE}})$  is the estimated maximum value of the likelihood function. The value of  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}_{\mathrm{MLE}}$  can be obtained using samples generated by a MCMC scheme. The BIC expression can be derived similarly to the Laplace's method, but this time with a second-order Taylor expansion of the log  $Z$  around its maximum  $\pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{MLE}}$  and a first-order expansion of the prior around  $\pmb{\theta}_{\mathrm{MLE}}$  [32, Ch. 9.1.3]. The derivation is given in the Supplementary Material. Then, the final approximation is

$$
Z \approx \widehat {Z} = \exp \left(\log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {M L E}}) - \frac {D _ {\theta}}{2} \log D _ {y}\right) = \exp \left(- \frac {1}{2} \mathrm {B I C}\right), \quad \text {a s} D _ {y} \rightarrow \infty , \tag {23}
$$

and BIC  $\approx -2\log Z$ , asymptotically as the number of data  $D_y$  grows. Then, smaller BIC values are associated to better models. Note that BIC clearly takes into account the complexity of the model since higher BIC values are given to models with more number of parameters  $D_{\theta}$ . Namely the penalty  $D_{\theta}\log D_y$  discourages overfitting, since increasing the number of parameters generally improves the goodness of the fit. Other criteria can be found in the literature, such as the well-known Akaike information criterion (AIC),

$$
\mathrm {A I C} = 2 D _ {\theta} - 2 \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \widehat {\pmb {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {M L E}}).
$$

However, they are not an approximation of the marginal likelihood  $Z$  and are usually founded on information theory derivations. Generally, they have the form of  $c_{p} - 2\log \ell (\mathbf{y}|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathrm{MLE}})$  where the

penalty term  $c_p$  of the model complexity changes in each different criterion (e.g.,  $c_p = D_\theta \log D_y$  in BIC and  $c_p = 2D_\theta$  in AIC). Another example that uses MCMC samples is the Deviance Information Criterion (DIC), i.e.,

$$
\mathrm {D I C} = - \frac {4}{N} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n}) - 2 \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \bar {\boldsymbol {\theta}}), \quad \text {w h e r e} \quad \bar {\boldsymbol {\theta}} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n}, \tag {24}
$$

and  $\{\pmb{\theta}_n\}_{n=1}^N$  are outputs of an MCMC algorithm [9]. In this case, note that  $c_p = -\frac{4}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log \ell(\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}_n)$ . DIC is considered more adequate for hierarchical models than AIC, BIC [9], but is not directly related to the marginal likelihood [11]. See also related comments in Section 2.2.4.

# 3.3 Kernel density estimation (KDE)

KDE can be used to approximate the value of the posterior density at a given point  $\pmb{\theta}^{*}$ , and then consider  $Z\approx \frac{\pi(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{y})}{\widehat{P}(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{y})}$ . For instance, we can build a kernel density estimate (KDE) of  $P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  based on  $M$  samples distributed according to the posterior (obtained via an MCMC algorithm, for instance) by using  $M$  normalized kernel functions  $k(\pmb {\theta}|\pmb {\mu}_m,h)$  (with  $\int_{\Theta}k(\pmb {\theta}|\pmb {\mu}_m,h)d\pmb {\theta} = 1$  for all  $m$ ) where  $\pmb{\mu}_{m}$  is a location parameter and  $h$  is a scale parameter,

$$
\widehat {P} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) = \frac {1}{M} \sum_ {m = 1} ^ {M} k \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \boldsymbol {\mu} _ {m}, h\right), \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\mu} _ {m} \right\} _ {m = 1} ^ {M} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \mathbf {y}) \quad \text {(e . g . , v i a M C M C)}. \tag {25}
$$

Generally,  $\widehat{P}(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{y})$  is a biased estimation of  $P(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{y})$ . The estimator is  $\widehat{Z} = \frac{\pi(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{y})}{\widehat{P}(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{y})}$  where the point  $\pmb{\theta}^{*}$  can be chosen as  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}_{\mathrm{MAP}}$ . If we consider  $N$  different points  $\pmb{\theta}_1,\dots,\pmb{\theta}_N$  (selected without any specific rule) we can also write a more general approximation,

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\widehat {P} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}. \tag {26}
$$

Remark 4. The estimator above is generally biased and depends on the choices of (a) of the points  $\pmb{\theta}_1, \dots, \pmb{\theta}_N$ , (b) the scale parameter  $h$ , and (c) the number of samples  $M$  for building  $\hat{P}(\pmb{\theta}^*|\mathbf{y})$ .

Remark 5. A improved version of this approximation can be obtained by the importance sampling approach described in Sect. 4, where  $\pmb{\theta}_1,\dots,\pmb{\theta}_N$  are drawn from the KDE mixture  $\widehat{P} (\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . In this case, the resulting estimator is unbiased.

# 3.4 Chib's method

In [25, 33], the authors present more sophisticated methods to estimate  $P(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{y})$  using outputs from Gibbs sampling and the Metropolis-Hastings (MH) algorithm respectively [2]. Here we only present the latter method, since it can be applied in more general settings. In [33], the authors

propose to estimate the value of the posterior at one point  $\pmb{\theta}^{*}$ , i.e.,  $P(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{y})$ , using the output from a MH sampler. More specifically, let us denote the current state as  $\pmb{\theta}$ . A possible candidate as future state  $\mathbf{z} \sim \varphi(\mathbf{z}|\pmb{\theta})$  (where  $\varphi(\mathbf{z}|\pmb{\theta})$  represents the proposal density used within MH), is accepted with probability  $\alpha(\pmb{\theta}, \mathbf{z}) = \min \left\{1, \frac{\pi(\mathbf{z}|\mathbf{y})\varphi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{z})}{\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})\varphi(\mathbf{z}|\pmb{\theta})}\right\}$  [2, 34]. This is just an example of  $\alpha(\pmb{\theta}, \mathbf{z})$  that by construction the probability  $\alpha$  satisfies the detailed balance condition [34, Section 2.4], [35], i.e.,

$$
\alpha (\boldsymbol {\theta}, \mathbf {z}) \varphi (\mathbf {z} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) = \alpha (\mathbf {z}, \boldsymbol {\theta}) \varphi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {z}) P (\mathbf {z} | \mathbf {y}). \tag {27}
$$

By integrating in  $\pmb{\theta}$  both sides, we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \int_ {\Theta} \alpha (\pmb {\theta}, \mathbf {z}) \varphi (\mathbf {z} | \pmb {\theta}) P (\pmb {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) d \pmb {\theta} = \int_ {\Theta} \alpha (\mathbf {z}, \pmb {\theta}) \varphi (\pmb {\theta} | \mathbf {z}) P (\mathbf {z} | \mathbf {y}) d \pmb {\theta}, \\ = P (\mathbf {z} | \mathbf {y}) \int_ {\Theta} \alpha (\mathbf {z}, \boldsymbol {\theta}) \varphi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {z}) d \boldsymbol {\theta}, \\ \end{array}
$$

hence finally we can solve with respect to  $P(\mathbf{z}|\mathbf{y})$  obtaining

$$
P (\mathbf {z} | \mathbf {y}) = \frac {\int_ {\Theta} \alpha (\boldsymbol {\theta} , \mathbf {z}) \varphi (\mathbf {z} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) d \boldsymbol {\theta}}{\int_ {\Theta} \alpha (\mathbf {z} , \boldsymbol {\theta}) \varphi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {z}) d \boldsymbol {\theta}}. \tag {28}
$$

This suggests the following estimate of  $P(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{y})$  at a specific point  $\pmb{\theta}^{*}$  (note that  $\pmb{\theta}^{*}$  plays the role of  $\mathbf{z}$  in the equation above),

$$
\widehat {P} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) = \frac {\frac {1}{N _ {1}} \sum_ {i = i} ^ {N _ {1}} \alpha \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} , \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}\right) \varphi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}{\frac {1}{N _ {2}} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \alpha \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}, \mathbf {v} _ {j}\right)}, \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \mathbf {y}), \left\{\mathbf {v} _ {j} \right\} _ {j = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \sim \varphi (\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}). \tag {29}
$$

The same outputs of the MH scheme can be considered as  $\{\pmb{\theta}_i\}_{i=1}^{N_1}$ . The final estimator is again  $\widehat{Z} = \frac{\pi(\pmb{\theta}^*|\mathbf{y})}{\widehat{P}(\pmb{\theta}^*|\mathbf{y})}$ , i.e.,

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) \frac {1}{N _ {2}} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \alpha \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}, \mathbf {v} _ {j}\right)}{\frac {1}{N _ {1}} \sum_ {i = i} ^ {N _ {1}} \alpha \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} , \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}\right) \varphi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}, \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \mathbf {y}), \left\{\mathbf {v} _ {j} \right\} _ {j = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \sim \varphi (\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}). \tag {30}
$$

The point  $\pmb{\theta}^{*}$  is usually chosen in an high probability region. Interesting discussions are contained in [36], where the authors also show that this estimator is related to bridge sampling idea described in Section 4.2. For more details, see Section 4.2.2.

# 3.5 Interpolative approaches

Another possibility is to approximate  $Z$  by substituting the true  $\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  with interpolation or a regression function  $\widehat{\pi}(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  in the integral (4). For simplicity, we focus on the interpolation case, but all the considerations can be easily extended for a regression scenario. Given a set of nodes

$\{\pmb{\theta}_1, \dots, \pmb{\theta}_N\} \subset \Theta$  and  $N$  nonlinear functions  $k(\pmb{\theta}, \pmb{\theta}') : \Theta \times \Theta \to \mathbb{R}$  chosen in advance by the user (generally, centered around  $\pmb{\theta}'$ ), we can build the interpolant of unnormalized posterior  $\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  as follows

$$
\widehat {\pi} (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \beta_ {i} k (\boldsymbol {\theta}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}), \tag {31}
$$

where  $\beta_{i}\in \mathbb{R}$  and the subindex  $u$  denotes that is an approximation of the unnormalized function  $\pi (\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . The coefficients  $\beta_{i}$  are chosen such that  $\widehat{\pi}_u(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  interpolates the points  $\{\pmb {\theta}_n,\pi (\pmb {\theta}_n|\mathbf{y})\}$ , that is,  $\widehat{\pi} (\pmb {\theta}_n|\mathbf{y}) = \pi (\pmb {\theta}_n|\mathbf{y})$ . Then, we desire that

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \beta_ {i} k (\pmb {\theta} _ {n}, \pmb {\theta} _ {i}) = \pi (\pmb {\theta} _ {n} | \mathbf {y}),
$$

for all  $n = 1, \dots, N$ . Hence, we can write a  $N \times N$  linear system where the  $\beta_{i}$  are the  $N$  unknowns, i.e.,

$$
\left( \begin{array}{c c c c} k (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}) & k (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {2}) & \dots & k (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {N}) \\ k (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {2}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}) & k (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {2}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {2}) & \dots & k (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {2}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {N}) \\ \vdots & & \ddots & \vdots \\ k (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {N}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}) & k (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {N}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {2}) & \dots & k (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {N}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {N}) \end{array} \right) \left( \begin{array}{c} \beta_ {1} \\ \beta_ {2} \\ \vdots \\ \beta_ {N} \end{array} \right) = \left( \begin{array}{c} \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} | \mathbf {y}) \\ \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {2} | \mathbf {y}) \\ \vdots \\ \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {N} | \mathbf {y}) \end{array} \right) \tag {32}
$$

In matrix form, we have

$$
\mathbf {K} \boldsymbol {\beta} = \mathbf {y}, \tag {33}
$$

where  $(\mathbf{K})_{i,j} = k(\pmb{\theta}_i, \pmb{\theta}_j)$  and  $\mathbf{y} = [\pi(\pmb{\theta}_1|\mathbf{y}), \dots, \pi(\pmb{\theta}_N|\mathbf{y})]^\top$ . Thus, the solution is  $\beta = \mathbf{K}^{-1}\mathbf{y}$ . Now the interpolant  $\widehat{\pi}_u(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}) = \sum_{i=1}^{N} \beta_i k(\pmb{\theta}, \pmb{\theta}_i)$  can be used to approximate  $Z$  as follows

$$
\widehat {Z} = \int_ {\Theta} \widehat {\pi} _ {u} (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \beta_ {i} \int_ {\Theta} k (\boldsymbol {\theta}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}) d \boldsymbol {\theta}. \tag {34}
$$

If we are able to compute analytically  $\int_{\Theta}k(\pmb {\theta},\pmb{\theta}_i)d\pmb{\theta}$ , we have an approximation  $\widehat{Z}$ . Some suitable choices of  $k(\cdot ,\cdot)$  are rectangular, triangular and Gaussian functions. More specifically, if all the nonlinearities  $k(\pmb {\theta},\pmb {\theta}_i)$  are normalized (i.e.  $\int_{\Theta}k(\pmb {\theta},\pmb {\theta}_i)d\pmb {\theta} = 1$ ), the approximation of  $Z$  is  $\widehat{Z} = \sum_{i = 1}^{N}\beta_{i}$ . This approach is related to the so-called Bayesian quadrature (using Gaussian process approximation) [37] and the sticky proposal constructions within MCMC or rejection sampling algorithms [38, 39, 40, 41]. Adaptive schemes adding sequentially more nodes could be also considered, improving the approximation  $\widehat{Z}$  [39, 40]. The quality of the interpolating approximation deteriorates as the dimension of  $\pmb{\theta}$  grows (see e.g. [42] for explicit error bounds).

# 4 Techniques based on IS

Most of the techniques for approximating the marginal likelihood are based on the importance sampling (IS) approach. Other methods are directly or indirectly related to the IS framework. In

this sense, this section is the core of this survey. The standard IS scheme relies on the following equality,

$$
\begin{array}{l} Z = \int_ {\Theta} \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} = \mathbb {E} _ {\bar {q}} \left[ \frac {\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y})}{\bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \right] = \int_ {\Theta} \frac {\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y})}{\bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} (35) \\ = \int_ {\Theta} \frac {\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) g (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{\bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) d \boldsymbol {\theta}, (36) \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\bar{q} (\pmb {\theta})$  is a simpler normalized proposal density,  $\int_{\Theta}\bar{q} (\pmb {\theta})d\pmb {\theta} = 1$

IS version 1. Drawing  $N$  independent samples from proposal  $\bar{q} (\pmb {\theta})$ , the unbiased IS estimator (denoted as IS vers-1) of  $Z$  is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widehat {Z} _ {I S 1} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\bar {q} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)} (37) \\ = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} w _ {i}, (38) \\ = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}) g (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}{\bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \rho_ {i} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}), \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta}), (39) \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $w_{i} = \frac{\pi(\pmb{\theta}_{i}|\mathbf{y})}{\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta}_{i})}$  are the standard IS weights and  $\rho_{i} = \frac{g(\pmb{\theta}_{i})}{\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta}_{i})}$ .

Optimal proposal in IS vers-1. The optimal proposal, in terms of mean square error (MSE), in the standard IS scheme above is  $\bar{q}^{\mathrm{opt}}(\pmb {\theta}) = P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y})$

IS version 2. An alternative IS estimator (denoted as IS vers-2) is given by, considering a possibly unnormalized proposal pdf  $q(\pmb{\theta}) \propto \bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  (the case  $q(\pmb{\theta}) = \bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  is also included),

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widehat {Z} _ {I S 2} = \frac {1}{\sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} \frac {g \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n}\right)}{q \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n}\right)}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {g \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}{q \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}), (40) \\ = \frac {1}{\sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} \rho_ {n}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \rho_ {i} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}), (41) \\ = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \bar {\rho} _ {i} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}), \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta}). (42) \\ \end{array}
$$

The estimator above is biased. However, it is a convex combination of likelihood values  $\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta}_i)$  since  $\sum_{i = 1}^{N}\bar{\rho}_{i} = 1$ . Hence, in this case  $\min_i\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta}_i)\leq \widehat{Z}\leq \max_i\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta}_i)$ , i.e., the estimator fulfills the bounds of  $Z$ , shown Section 2.2. Moreover, the estimator allows the use of an unnormalized proposal pdf  $q(\pmb {\theta})\propto \bar{q} (\pmb {\theta})$  and  $\rho_{i} = \frac{g(\pmb{\theta}_{i})}{q(\pmb{\theta}_{i})}$ . For instance, one could consider  $\bar{q} (\pmb {\theta}) = P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ , i.e.,

generate samples  $\{\pmb {\theta}_i\}_{i = 1}^N\sim P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  by an MCMC algorithm and then evaluate  $\rho_{i} = \frac{g(\pmb{\theta}_{i})}{\pi(\pmb{\theta}_{i}|\mathbf{y})}$

Optimal proposal in IS vers-2. The optimal proposal, in terms of MSE, for the IS vers-2 is  $\bar{q}^{\mathrm{opt}}(\pmb {\theta})\propto |P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y}) - g(\pmb {\theta})|$

Table 3 summarizes the IS estimators and shows some important special cases that will be described in the next section.

Table 3: IS estimators Eqs. (37)-(40) and relevant special cases.  

<table><tr><td colspan="6">\(\widehat{Z}_{IS1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{g(\boldsymbol{\theta}_i)}{\bar{q}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_i)}\ell(\mathbf{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_i)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho_i\ell(\mathbf{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_i),\quad\rho_i=\frac{g(\boldsymbol{\theta}_i)}{\bar{q}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_i)}\)</td></tr><tr><td>Name</td><td>Estimator</td><td>q(θ)</td><td>\(\bar{q}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\)</td><td>Need of MCMC</td><td>Unbiased</td></tr><tr><td>Naive Monte Carlo</td><td>\(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell(\mathbf{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_i)\)</td><td>g(θ)</td><td>g(θ)</td><td>-</td><td>✓</td></tr><tr><td colspan="6">\(\widehat{Z}_{IS2}=\frac{1}{\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{g(\boldsymbol{\theta}_n)}{q(\boldsymbol{\theta}_n)}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{g(\boldsymbol{\theta}_i)}{q(\boldsymbol{\theta}_i)}\ell(\mathbf{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_i)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{\rho}_i\ell(\mathbf{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_i)\)</td></tr><tr><td>Name</td><td>Estimator</td><td>q(θ)</td><td>\(\bar{q}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\)</td><td>Need of MCMC</td><td>Unbiased</td></tr><tr><td>Naive Monte Carlo Harmonic mean</td><td>\(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\ell(\mathbf{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_i)\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{1}{\ell(\mathbf{y}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_i)}\right)^{-1}\)</td><td>g(θ)π(θ|y)</td><td>g(θ)P(θ|y)</td><td>-✓</td><td>✓-</td></tr></table>

Different sub-families of IS schemes are commonly used for computing normalizing constants [43, chapter 5]. A first approach uses draws from a proposal density  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  that is completely known (i.e. direct sampling and evaluate). Sophisticated choices of  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  frequently imply the use of MCMC algorithms to sample from  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  and that we can only evaluate  $q(\pmb{\theta}) \propto \bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$ . The one-proposal approach is described in Section 4.1. A second class is formed by methods which use more than one proposal density or a mixture of them (see Sections 4.2, 4.3 and 5). Moreover, adaptive importance sampling (AIS) schemes are often designed, where the proposal (or the cloud of proposals) is improved during some iterations, in some way such that  $\bar{q}_t(\pmb{\theta})$  (where  $t$  is an iteration index) becomes closer and closer to the optima proposal  $q^{\mathrm{opt}}(\pmb{\theta})$ . For more details, see the reviews in [44]. Some AIS methods, obtained combining MCMC and IS approaches, are described in Section 5.

# 4.1 Techniques using draws from one proposal density

In this section, all the techniques are IS schemes which use a unique proposal pdf, and are based on the identity Eq. (35). The techniques differ in the choice of  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$ . Recall that the optimal proposal choice for IS vers-1 is  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta}) = P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}) = \frac{1}{Z}\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . This choice is clearly difficult for two reasons: (a) we have to draw from  $P$  and (b) we do not know  $Z$ , hence we cannot evaluate  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$

but only  $q(\pmb{\theta}) = \pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  (where  $q(\pmb{\theta}) \propto \bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$ ). However, there are some methods based on this idea, as shown in the following. The techniques below are enumerated in an increasing order of complexity.

Naive Monte Carlo (arithmetic mean estimator). It is straightforward to note that the integral above can be expressed as  $Z = \mathbb{E}_g[\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta})]$ , then we can draw  $N$  samples  $\{\pmb {\theta}_i\}_{i = 1}^N$  from the prior  $g(\pmb {\theta})$  and compute the following estimator

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}), \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim g (\boldsymbol {\theta}). \tag {43}
$$

Namely a simple average of the likelihoods of a sample from the prior. Note that  $\widehat{Z}$  will be very inefficient (large variance) if the posterior is much more concentrated than the prior (i.e., small overlap between likelihood and prior pdfs). Therefore, alternatives have been proposed, see below. It is a special case of the IS estimator with the choice  $\bar{q} (\pmb {\theta}) = g(\pmb {\theta})$  (i.e., the proposal pdf is the prior).

Harmonic mean (HM) estimators. The HM estimator can be directly derived from the following expected value,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {P} \left[ \frac {1}{\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta})} \right] = \int_ {\Theta} \frac {1}{\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta})} P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) d \boldsymbol {\theta}, (44) \\ = \frac {1}{Z} \int_ {\Theta} \frac {1}{\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta})} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) g (\boldsymbol {\theta}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} = \frac {1}{Z} \int_ {\Theta} g (\boldsymbol {\theta}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} = \frac {1}{Z}. (45) \\ \end{array}
$$

The main idea is again to use the posterior itself as proposal. Since direct sampling from  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  is generally impossible, this task requires the use of MCMC algorithms. Thus, the HM estimator is

$$
\widehat {Z} = \left(\frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {1}{\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}\right) ^ {- 1} = \frac {1}{\frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {1}{\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}}, \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) \text {(v i a M C M C)}. \tag {46}
$$

The HM estimator converges almost surely to the correct value, but the variance of  $\widehat{Z}$  is often high and possibly infinite. The HM estimator is a special case of Reverse Importance Sampling (RIS) below.

Reverse Importance Sampling (RIS). The RIS scheme [45], also known as reciprocal IS, can be derived from the identity

$$
\frac {1}{Z} = \mathbb {E} _ {P} \left[ \frac {f (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y})} \right] = \int_ {\Theta} \frac {f (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y})} P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} \tag {47}
$$

where we consider an auxiliary normalized function  $f(\pmb{\theta})$ , i.e.,  $\int_{\Theta} f(\pmb{\theta}) d\pmb{\theta} = 1$ . Then, one could consider the estimator

$$
\widehat {Z} = \left(\frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {f \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}{\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}\right) ^ {- 1} = \left(\frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {f \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}{\ell \left(\mathbf {y} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right) g \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}\right) ^ {- 1}, \quad \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) \text {(v i a M C M C)}. \tag {48}
$$

The estimator above is consistent but biased. Indeed, the expression  $\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{f(\pmb{\theta}_i)}{\pi(\pmb{\theta}_i|\mathbf{y})}$  is a unbiased estimator of  $1/Z$ , but  $\widehat{Z}$  in the Eq. (48) is not an unbiased estimator of  $Z$ . Note that  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  plays the role of importance density from which we need to draw from. Therefore, another sampling technique must be used (such as a MCMC method) in order to generate samples from  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . In this case, we do not need samples from  $f(\pmb{\theta})$ , although its choice affects the precision of the approximation. Unlike in the standard IS approach,  $f(\pmb{\theta})$  must have lighter tails than  $\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}) = \ell(\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta})g(\pmb{\theta})$ . For further details, see the example in Section 8.1. Finally, note that the HM estimator is a special case of RIS when  $f(\pmb{\theta}) = g(\pmb{\theta})$  in Eq. (48). In [46], the authors propose taking  $f(\pmb{\theta})$  that is uniform in a high posterior density region whereas, in [47], they consider taking  $f(\pmb{\theta})$  to be a piecewise constant function.

# 4.1.1 The pre-umbrella estimators

All the estimators that we have seen so far can be unified within a common formulation, considering the more general problem of estimating a ratio of two normalizing constants  $c_{1} / c_{2}$ , where  $c_{i} = \int q_{i}(\pmb{\theta}) d\pmb{\theta}$  and  $\bar{q}_{i}(\pmb{\theta}) = q_{i}(\pmb{\theta}) / c_{i}$ ,  $i = 1, 2$ . Assuming we can evaluate both  $q_{1}(\pmb{\theta})$ ,  $q_{2}(\pmb{\theta})$ , and draw samples from one of them, say  $\bar{q}_{2}(\pmb{\theta})$ , the importance sampling estimator of ratio  $c_{1} / c_{2}$  is

$$
\frac {c _ {1}}{c _ {2}} = \mathbb {E} _ {\bar {q} _ {2}} \left[ \frac {q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \right] \approx \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {q _ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}{q _ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}, \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim \bar {q} _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta}). \tag {49}
$$

Remark 6. The relative MSE (rel-MSE) of (49), in estimation of the ratio  $r = \frac{c_1}{c_2}$ , i.e., rel-MSE =  $\frac{\mathbb{E}[(\hat{r} - r)^2]}{r^2}$ , is given by rel-MSE =  $\frac{1}{N}\chi^2 (\bar{q}_1||\bar{q}_2)$ , where  $\chi^2 (\bar{q}_1||\bar{q}_2)$  is the Pearson divergence between  $\bar{q}_1$  and  $\bar{q}_2$  [48].

This framework includes almost all the estimators discussed so far in this section, as shown in Table 4. However, the IS vers-2 estimator is not a special case of Eq. (49).

Below we consider an extension of Eq. (49) where an additional density  $\bar{q}_3(\pmb{\theta})$  is employed for generating samples.

# 4.1.2 Umbrella Sampling (a.k.a. ratio importance sampling)

The IS estimator of  $c_{1} / c_{2}$  given in Eq. (49) may be inefficient when there is little overlap between  $\bar{q}_{1}(\pmb{\theta})$  and  $\bar{q}_{2}(\pmb{\theta})$ , i.e., when  $\int_{\Theta} \bar{q}_{1}(\pmb{\theta}) \bar{q}_{2}(\pmb{\theta}) d\pmb{\theta}$  is small. Umbrella sampling (originally proposed in the computational physics literature, [49]; also studied under the name ratio importance sampling

Table 4: Summary of techniques considering the expression (49).  

<table><tr><td>Name</td><td>q1(θ)</td><td>q2(θ)</td><td>c1</td><td>c2</td><td>Proposal pdf q̅2(θ)</td><td>c1/c2</td></tr><tr><td>IS vers-1</td><td>π(θ|y)</td><td>q̅(θ)</td><td>Z</td><td>1</td><td>q̅(θ)</td><td>Z</td></tr><tr><td>Naive Monte Carlo</td><td>π(θ|y)</td><td>g(θ)</td><td>Z</td><td>1</td><td>g(θ)</td><td>Z</td></tr><tr><td>Harmonic mean</td><td>g(θ)</td><td>π(θ|y)</td><td>1</td><td>Z</td><td>P(θ|y)</td><td>1/Z</td></tr><tr><td>RIS</td><td>f(θ)</td><td>π(θ|y)</td><td>1</td><td>Z</td><td>P(θ|y)</td><td>1/Z</td></tr></table>

in [48]) is based on the identity

$$
\frac {c _ {1}}{c _ {2}} = \frac {c _ {1} / c _ {3}}{c _ {2} / c _ {3}} = \frac {\mathbb {E} _ {\bar {q} _ {3}} \left[ \frac {q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{q _ {3} (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \right]}{\mathbb {E} _ {\bar {q} _ {3}} \left[ \frac {q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{q _ {3} (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \right]} \approx \frac {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {q _ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}{q _ {3} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}}{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {q _ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}{q _ {3} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}}, \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim \bar {q} _ {3} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \tag {50}
$$

where  $\bar{q}_3(\pmb{\theta}) \propto q_3(\pmb{\theta})$  represents a middle density. A good choice of  $\bar{q}_3(\pmb{\theta})$  should have large overlaps with both  $\bar{q}_i(\pmb{\theta})$ ,  $i = 1,2$ . The performance of umbrella sampling clearly depends on the choice of  $\bar{q}_3(\pmb{\theta})$ . Note that, when  $\bar{q}_3 = \bar{q}_2$ , we recover Eq. (49).

Optimal umbrella proposal. The optimal umbrella sampling density  $\bar{q}_3^{\mathrm{opt}}(\pmb {\theta})$ , that minimizes the asymptotic relative mean-square error, is

$$
\bar {q} _ {3} ^ {\text {o p t}} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) = \frac {| \bar {q} _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) - \bar {q} _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) |}{\int | \bar {q} _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\prime}) - \bar {q} _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\prime}) | d \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\prime}} = \frac {| q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) - \frac {c _ {1}}{c _ {2}} q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) |}{\int | q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\prime}) - \frac {c _ {1}}{c _ {2}} q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\prime}) | d \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\prime}}. \tag {51}
$$

Remark 7. The rel-MSE in estimation of the ratio  $\frac{c_1}{c_2}$  of the optimal umbrella estimator, with  $N$  great enough, is given by rel-MSE  $\approx \frac{1}{N} L_1^2 (\bar{q}_1,\bar{q}_2)$ , where  $L_{1}^{2}(\bar{q}_{1},\bar{q}_{2})$  denotes the  $L_{1}$ -distance between  $\bar{q}_{1}$  and  $\bar{q}_{2}$  [48, Theorem 3.2]. Moreover, since  $L_{1}^{2}(\bar{q}_{1},\bar{q}_{2}) \leq \chi^{2}(\bar{q}_{1}||\bar{q}_{2})$ , the optimal umbrella estimator is asymptotically more efficient than the estimator (49) [50, Sect. 3].

Two-stage umbrella sampling. Since this  $\bar{q}_3^{\mathrm{opt}}(\pmb {\theta})$  depends on the unknown ratio  $\frac{c_1}{c_2}$ , it is not available for a direct use. The following two-stage procedure is often used in practice:

1. Stage 1: Draw  $N_{1}$  samples from an arbitrary density  $\bar{q}_{3}^{(1)}(\pmb{\theta})$  and use them to obtain

$$
\widehat {r} ^ {(1)} = \frac {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \frac {q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}{q _ {3} ^ {(1)} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}}{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \frac {q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}{q _ {3} ^ {(1)} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}}, \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \sim \bar {q} _ {3} ^ {(1)} (\boldsymbol {\theta}). \tag {52}
$$

and define

$$
\bar {q} _ {3} ^ {(2)} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \propto | q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) - \widehat {r} ^ {(1)} q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) |. \tag {53}
$$

2. Stage 2: Draw  $N_{2}$  samples from  $\bar{q}_{3}^{(2)}(\pmb{\theta})$  via MCMC and define the umbrella sampling estimator  $\hat{r}^{(2)}$  of  $\frac{c_1}{c_2}$  as follows

$$
\hat {r} ^ {(2)} = \frac {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n _ {2}} \frac {q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}{q _ {3} ^ {(2)} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}}{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n _ {2}} \frac {q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}{q _ {3} ^ {(2)} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}}, \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {n _ {2}} \sim \bar {q} _ {3} ^ {(2)} (\boldsymbol {\theta}). \tag {54}
$$

Remark 8. The number of stages could be increased considering, at each  $t$ -th stage, the proposal  $\bar{q}_3^{(t)}(\pmb{\theta}) \propto |q_1(\pmb{\theta}) - \hat{r}^{(t-1)}q_2(\pmb{\theta})|$  and obtaining a new estimation  $\hat{r}^{(t)}$ . In this case, we have an umbrella scheme with adaptive proposal  $\bar{q}_3^{(t)}(\pmb{\theta})$ .

# 4.1.3 Umbrella for  $Z$ : the self-normalized Importance Sampling (Self-IS)

Here, we describe an important special case of the umbrella sampling approach. Considering the umbrella identity (50) an setting  $q_{1}(\pmb{\theta}) = \pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ ,  $q_{2}(\pmb{\theta}) = \bar{q}_{2}(\pmb{\theta}) = f(\pmb{\theta})$ ,  $c_{1} = Z$ ,  $c_{2} = 1$  and  $c_{3} \in \mathbb{R}$ , we obtain

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {1}{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {f (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}{q _ {3} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} | \mathbf {y})}{q _ {3} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}. \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim \bar {q} _ {3} (\boldsymbol {\theta}). \tag {55}
$$

which is called the self-normalized IS (Self-IS) estimator. Note that  $f(\pmb{\theta})$  is an auxiliary normalized pdf, but we draw samples from  $\bar{q}_3(\pmb{\theta})$ . In order to understand the reason of its name is interesting to derive it with standard IS arguments. Let us consider that our proposal  $q(\pmb{\theta})$  in the standard IS scheme is not normalized, and we can evaluate it up to a normalizing constant  $q(\pmb{\theta}) \propto \bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$ . We also denote  $c = \int_{\Theta} q(\pmb{\theta}) d\pmb{\theta}$ . Note that this also occurs in the ideal case of using  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta}) = P(\pmb{\theta} | \mathbf{y}) = \frac{1}{Z} \pi(\pmb{\theta} | \mathbf{y})$  where  $c = Z$  and  $q(\pmb{\theta}) = \pi(\pmb{\theta} | \mathbf{y})$ . In this case, we have

$$
\frac {\widehat {Z}}{c} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} | \mathbf {y})}{q (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}, \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta}). \tag {56}
$$

Therefore, we need an additional estimation of  $c$ . We can also use IS for this goal, considering a new normalized reference function  $f(\pmb{\theta})$ , i.e.,  $\int_{\Theta} f(\pmb{\theta}) d\pmb{\theta} = 1$ . Now,

$$
\frac {1}{c} = E _ {\bar {q}} \left[ \frac {f (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{q (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \right] = \int_ {\Theta} \frac {f (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{q (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} \approx \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {f (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}{q (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}, \quad \{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta}). \tag {57}
$$

Replacing (57) into (56), we obtain the self-normalized IS estimator in Eq. (55), i.e.,  $\widehat{Z} = \frac{1}{\sum_{i=1}^{N} \frac{f(\pmb{\theta}_i)}{q(\pmb{\theta}_i)}} \sum_{i=1}^{N} \frac{\pi(\pmb{\theta}_i | \mathbf{y})}{q(\pmb{\theta}_i)}$  with  $\{\pmb{\theta}_i\}_{i=1}^N \sim \bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$ .

The HM estimator is also a special case of Self-IS setting again  $f(\pmb{\theta}) = g(\pmb{\theta})$  and  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta}) = P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ , so that  $q(\pmb{\theta}) = \pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . Moreover, the RIS estimator is a special case of the Self-IS estimator above when  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta}) = P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  and  $q(\pmb{\theta}) = \pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ .

Optimal self-IS (O-Self-IS). Since the Self-IS estimator is a special case of umbrella sampling, the optimal proposal in this case is  $\bar{q}^{\mathrm{opt}}(\pmb {\theta})\propto |P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y}) - f(\pmb {\theta})|$ , and the optimal estimator is

$$
\widehat {Z} _ {\mathrm {O - S e l f - I S}} = \frac {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}{\left| P \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) - f \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right) \right|}}{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {f \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}{\left| P \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) - f \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right) \right|}}, \quad \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \sim \bar {q} ^ {\text {o p t}} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \propto | P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) - f (\boldsymbol {\theta}) |. \tag {58}
$$

Since the density cannot be evaluated (and also is not easy to draw from), this estimator is not of direct use and we need to resort to the two-stage procedure that we discussed above. Due to Remark 7, the O-Self-IS estimator is asymptotically more efficient than IS vers-1 estimator using  $f(\pmb{\theta})$  as proposal, i.e., drawing samples from  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta}) = f(\pmb{\theta})$ .

# 4.1.4 Summary

The more general expressions are the two identities (49)-(50) for estimating a ratio of normalizing constants  $\frac{c_1}{c_2}$ . The umbrella identity (50) is the more general since three densities are involved, and contains the Eq. (49) as special case when  $q_{3}(\pmb{\theta}) = q_{2}(\pmb{\theta})$ . The Self-IS estimator coincides with the umbrella estimator when we approximate only one normalizing constant,  $Z$  (i.e., for  $\frac{c_1}{c_2} = Z$ ). Therefore, regarding the estimation of only one constant  $Z$ , the Self-IS estimator has the more general form and includes the rest of estimators as special cases. All these connections are summarized in Table 5. Finally, Table 6 provides another summary of the one-proposal estimators of  $Z$ . Note that in the standard IS estimator the option  $\bar{q} (\pmb{\theta}) = P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  is not feasible, whereas it is possible for its second version.
