# 4.2 Techniques using draws from two proposal densities

In the previous section, we considered estimators of  $Z$  that use samples drawn from a single proposal density. More specifically, we have described several IS schemes using a generic pdf  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  or  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  as proposal density. In this section, we introduce schemes where  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  and  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  are employed jointly. More generally, we consider estimators of a ratio of constants,  $\frac{c_2}{c_1}$ , that employ samples from two proposal densities, denoted as  $\bar{q}_i(\pmb{\theta}) = \frac{q_i(\pmb{\theta})}{c_i}, i = 1,2$ . Note that drawing  $N_1$  samples from  $\bar{q}_1(\pmb{\theta})$  and  $N_2$  samples from  $\bar{q}_2(\pmb{\theta})$  is equivalent to sampling by a deterministic mixture approach from the mixture  $\bar{q}_{mix}(\pmb{\theta}) = \frac{N_1}{N_1 + N_2}\bar{q}_1(\pmb{\theta}) + \frac{N_2}{N_1 + N_2}\bar{q}_2(\pmb{\theta})$ , i.e., a single density defined as a mixture of two pdfs [51]. Thus, methods drawing from a mixture of two pdfs as  $\bar{q}_{mix}(\pmb{\theta})$ , are also considered in this section.

# 4.2.1 Bridge sampling identity

All the techniques, that we will describe below, are based on the following bridge sampling identity [52],

$$
\frac {c _ {1}}{c _ {2}} = \frac {\mathbb {E} _ {\bar {q} _ {2}} [ q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \alpha (\boldsymbol {\theta}) ]}{\mathbb {E} _ {\bar {q} _ {1}} [ q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \alpha (\boldsymbol {\theta}) ]}. \tag {59}
$$

where  $\alpha(\pmb{\theta})$  is an arbitrary function defined on the intersection of the supports of  $\bar{q}_1$  and  $\bar{q}_2$ . Note that the expression above is an extension of the Eq. (49). Indeed, taking  $\alpha(\pmb{\theta}) = \frac{1}{q_2(\pmb{\theta})}$ , we recover Eq. (49). The identity in Eq. (59) and the umbrella identity in Eq. (50) are both useful when  $\bar{q}_1$  and  $\bar{q}_2$  have little overlap, i.e.,  $\int_{\Theta} \bar{q}_1(\pmb{\theta}) \bar{q}_2(\pmb{\theta}) d\pmb{\theta}$  is small. Moreover, if we set  $q_1(\pmb{\theta}) = \pi(\pmb{\theta} | \mathbf{y})$ ,  $c_1 = Z$ ,  $q_2(\pmb{\theta}) = \bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  and  $c_2 = 1$ , then the identity becomes

$$
Z = \frac {\mathbb {E} _ {\bar {q}} \left[ \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) \alpha (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \right]}{\mathbb {E} _ {P} \left[ \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \alpha (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \right]}. \tag {60}
$$

The corresponding estimator employs samples from both  $\bar{q}$  and  $P$ , i.e.,

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {\frac {1}{N _ {2}} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \alpha (\mathbf {z} _ {j}) \pi (\mathbf {z} _ {j} | \mathbf {y})}{\frac {1}{N _ {1}} \sum_ {i = i} ^ {N _ {1}} \alpha (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}) \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}, \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}), \quad \left\{\mathbf {z} _ {j} \right\} _ {j = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \sim \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta}). \tag {61}
$$

Figure 1 summarizes the connections among the Eqs. (49), (59), (60) and the corresponding different methods. The standard IS and RIS schemes have been described in the previous sections, whereas the corresponding locally-restricted versions will be introduced below.

![](images/d24fa27bf9469030bdfbcfd53a4acb09f33f9db99e0b291292fd41d70cbcd974.jpg)  
Figure 1: Graphical representation of the relationships among the Eqs. (49) (pre-umbrella identity), (59) (general bridge sampling identity), (60) (bridge sampling for  $Z$ ) and the corresponding different methods, starting from bridge sampling identity (59).

# 4.2.2 Relationship with Chib's method

The Chib estimator, described in Section 3.4, is

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) \frac {1}{N _ {2}} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \alpha \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}, \mathbf {v} _ {j}\right)}{\frac {1}{N _ {1}} \sum_ {i = i} ^ {N _ {1}} \alpha \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} , \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}\right) \varphi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}, \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \mathbf {y}), \left\{\mathbf {v} _ {j} \right\} _ {j = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \sim \varphi (\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}), \tag {62}
$$

where  $\varphi (\pmb {\theta}|\pmb{\theta}^{*})$  is the proposal used inside an MCMC algorithm,  $\alpha (\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}):\mathbb{R}^{D_{\theta}}\times \mathbb{R}^{D_{\theta}}\to \mathbb{R}^{+}$  represents acceptance probability of this MCMC scheme and the point  $\pmb{\theta}^{*}$  is usually chosen in a high probability region. Note that the balance condition involving the function  $\varphi$ ,  $P$  and  $\alpha$  must be satisfied,

$$
\alpha (\boldsymbol {\theta}, \mathbf {z}) \varphi (\mathbf {z} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) = \alpha (\mathbf {z}, \boldsymbol {\theta}) \varphi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {z}) \pi (\mathbf {z} | \mathbf {y}).
$$

Using the balance condition above, if we replace  $\alpha (\pmb{\theta}^{*},\mathbf{v}_{j}) = \frac{\alpha(\mathbf{v}_{j},\pmb{\theta}^{*})\varphi(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{v}_{j})\pi(\mathbf{v}_{j}|\mathbf{y})}{\varphi(\mathbf{v}_{j}|\pmb{\theta}^{*})\pi(\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\mathbf{y})}$  inside the numerator of (62), we obtain

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {\frac {1}{N _ {2}} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \frac {\varphi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \mathbf {v} _ {j}\right)}{\varphi \left(\mathbf {v} _ {j} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}\right)} \alpha \left(\mathbf {v} _ {j} , \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}\right) \pi \left(\mathbf {v} _ {j} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\frac {1}{N _ {1}} \sum_ {i = i} ^ {N _ {1}} \alpha \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} , \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}\right) \varphi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}, \tag {63}
$$

and if we also assume a symmetric proposal  $\varphi (\pmb {\theta}|\pmb{\theta}^{*}) = \varphi (\pmb{\theta}^{*}|\pmb {\theta})$ , we can finally write

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {\frac {1}{N _ {2}} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \alpha \left(\mathbf {v} _ {j} , \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}\right) \pi \left(\mathbf {v} _ {j} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\frac {1}{N _ {1}} \sum_ {i = i} ^ {N _ {1}} \alpha \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} , \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}\right) \varphi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}\right)}, \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \mathbf {y}), \quad \left\{\mathbf {v} _ {j} \right\} _ {j = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \sim \varphi (\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*}), \tag {64}
$$

We can observe a clear connection between the estimators (61) and (62). Clearly,  $\varphi(\pmb{\theta}|\pmb{\theta}^*)$  plays the role of  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  in (61), and the acceptance function  $\alpha(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})$  plays the role of the  $\alpha$  function in (61). However, in this case,  $\varphi(\pmb{\theta}|\pmb{\theta}^*)$  participates also inside the MCMC used for generating  $\{\pmb{\theta}_i\}_{i=1}^{N_1} \sim P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . The function  $\alpha$  takes also part to the generation MCMC chain,  $\{\pmb{\theta}_i\}_{i=1}^{N_1}$  (being the acceptance probability of the new states), and generally its evaluation involves the evaluation of  $\varphi$  and  $P$ . Note also that (62) is more generic than (64), being valid also for non-symmetric proposals  $\varphi$ . For further discussion see [36].

# 4.2.3 Locally-restricted IS and RIS

In the literature, there exist variants of the estimators in Eqs. (43) and (46). These corrected estimators are attempts to improve the efficiency (e.g., remove the infinite variance cases, specially in the harmonic estimator) by restricting the integration to a smaller subset of  $\Theta$  (usually chosen in high posterior/likelihood-valued regions) generally denoted by  $\mathcal{B} \subset \Theta$ . As an example,  $\mathcal{B}$  can be a rectangular or ellipsoidal region centered at the maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimate  $\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{MAP}}$ .

Locally-restricted IS estimator. Consider the posterior mass of subset  $\mathcal{B} \subset \Theta$ ,

$$
Z _ {\mathcal {B}} = \int_ {\mathcal {B}} P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} = \int_ {\Theta} \mathbb {I} _ {\mathcal {B}} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \frac {\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) g (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{Z} d \boldsymbol {\theta}, \tag {65}
$$

where  $\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{B}}(\pmb{\theta})$  is an indicator function, taking value 1 for  $\pmb{\theta} \in \mathcal{B}$  and 0 otherwise. It leads to the following representation

$$
Z = \frac {1}{Z _ {\mathcal {B}}} \int_ {\Theta} \mathbb {I} _ {\mathcal {B}} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) g (\boldsymbol {\theta}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} = \frac {1}{Z _ {\mathcal {B}}} \mathbb {E} _ {\bar {q}} \left[ \mathbb {I} _ {\mathcal {B}} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \frac {\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) g (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{\bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \right]. \tag {66}
$$

We can estimate  $Z_{\mathcal{B}}$  considering  $N_{1}$  samples from  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  by taking the proportion of samples inside  $\mathcal{B}$ . The resulting locally-restricted IS estimator of  $Z$  is

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {\frac {1}{N _ {1}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \frac {\mathbb {I} _ {\mathcal {B}} (\mathbf {z} _ {i}) \ell (\mathbf {y} | \mathbf {z} _ {i}) g (\mathbf {z} _ {i})}{\bar {q} (\mathbf {z} _ {i})}}{\frac {1}{N _ {2}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \mathbb {I} _ {\mathcal {B}} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}, \quad \left\{\mathbf {z} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \sim \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta}), \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) \quad \text {(v i a M C M C)}. \tag {67}
$$

Note that the above estimator requires samples from two densities, namely the proposal  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  and the posterior density  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  (via MCMC).

Locally-restricted RIS estimator. To derive the locally-restricted RIS estimator, consider the mass of  $\mathcal{B}$  under  $\bar{q} (\pmb {\theta})$

$$
\bar {Q} (\mathcal {B}) = \int_ {\mathcal {B}} \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} = Z \cdot \mathbb {E} _ {P} \left[ \mathbb {I} _ {\mathcal {B}} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \frac {\bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) g (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \right], \tag {68}
$$

which leads to the following representation

$$
Z = \frac {\bar {Q} (\mathcal {B})}{\mathbb {E} _ {P} \left[ \frac {\mathbb {I} _ {\mathcal {B}} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) g (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \right]}. \tag {69}
$$

$\bar{Q} (\mathcal{B})$  can be estimated using a sample from  $\bar{q} (\pmb {\theta})$  by taking the proportion of sampled values inside  $\mathcal{B}$ . The locally-restricted RIS estimator is

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {\frac {1}{N _ {1}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \mathbb {I} _ {\mathcal {B}} (\mathbf {z} _ {i})}{\frac {1}{N _ {2}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \frac {\mathbb {I} _ {\mathcal {B}} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}) \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}{\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}) g (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}}, \quad \left\{\mathbf {z} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \sim \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta}), \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}). \tag {70}
$$

Other variants, where  $\mathcal{B}$  corresponds to highest density regions, can be found in [46].

# 4.2.4 Optimal construction of bridge sampling

Identities as (59) are associated to the bridge sampling approach. However, considering  $\alpha(\pmb{\theta}) = \frac{q_3(\pmb{\theta})}{q_2(\pmb{\theta}) q_1(\pmb{\theta})}$  in Eq. (59), bridge sampling can be also motivated from the expression

$$
\frac {c _ {1}}{c _ {2}} = \frac {c _ {3} / c _ {2}}{c _ {3} / c _ {1}} = \frac {\mathbb {E} _ {\bar {Q} _ {2}} \left[ \frac {q _ {3} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \right]}{\mathbb {E} _ {\bar {Q} _ {1}} \left[ \frac {q _ {3} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \right]}, \tag {71}
$$

where the density  $\bar{q}_3(\pmb{\theta}) \propto q_3(\pmb{\theta})$  is in some sense "in between"  $q_1(\pmb{\theta})$  and  $q_2(\pmb{\theta})$ . That is, instead of applying directly (49) to  $\frac{c_1}{c_2}$ , we apply it to first estimate  $\frac{c_3}{c_2}$  and  $\frac{c_3}{c_1}$ , and then take the ratio to cancel  $c_3$ . The bridge sampling estimator of  $\frac{c_1}{c_2}$  is then

$$
\frac {c _ {1}}{c _ {2}} \approx \frac {\frac {1}{N _ {2}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \frac {q _ {3} (\mathbf {z} _ {i})}{q _ {2} (\mathbf {z} _ {i})}}{\frac {1}{N _ {1}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \frac {q _ {3} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}{q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}}, \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \sim \bar {q} _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta}), \quad \left\{\mathbf {z} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \sim \bar {q} _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta}). \tag {72}
$$

Remark 9. We do not need to draw samples from  $\bar{q}_3(\pmb{\theta})$ , but only evaluate  $q_3(\pmb{\theta})$ . For a comparison with umbrella sampling see Table 7.

Table 7: Joint use of three densities: comparison between bridge and umbrella sampling.  

<table><tr><td>Method</td><td>q1(θ)</td><td>q3(θ)</td><td>q2(θ)</td><td>Identity</td></tr><tr><td>Umbrella sampling</td><td>evaluate</td><td>draw from</td><td>evaluate</td><td>c1/c2=c1/c3- (50)</td></tr><tr><td>Bridge sampling</td><td>draw from</td><td>evaluate</td><td>draw from</td><td>c1/c2=c3/c2/c3- (71)</td></tr></table>

Optimal bridge density. It can be shown that the optimal bridge density  $\bar{q}_3(\pmb{\theta})$  can be expressed as a weighted harmonic mean of  $\bar{q}_1(\pmb{\theta})$  and  $\bar{q}_2(\pmb{\theta})$  (with weights being the sampling rates),

$$
\begin{array}{l} \bar {q} _ {3} ^ {\mathrm {o p t}} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) = \frac {1}{\frac {N _ {2}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} [ \bar {q} _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) ] ^ {- 1} + \frac {N _ {1}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} [ \bar {q} _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) ] ^ {- 1}} \\ = \frac {1}{c _ {2}} \cdot \frac {N _ {1} + N _ {2}}{N _ {2} \frac {c _ {1}}{c _ {2}} q _ {1} ^ {- 1} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) + N _ {1} q _ {2} ^ {- 1} (\boldsymbol {\theta})} \\ \propto q _ {3} ^ {\text {o p t}} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) = \frac {q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{N _ {1} q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) + N _ {2} \frac {c _ {1}}{c _ {2}} q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}. \tag {73} \\ \end{array}
$$

This is an optimal bridge density if both  $N_{i}$  are strictly positive,  $N_{i} > 0$ , hence we draw from both  $\bar{q}_i(\pmb{\theta})$ . Note that  $\bar{q}_3^{\mathrm{opt}}(\pmb{\theta})$  depends on the unknown ratio  $r = \frac{c_1}{c_2}$ . Therefore, we cannot even evaluate  $q_3^{\mathrm{opt}}(\pmb{\theta})$ . Hence, we need to resort to the following iterative procedure to approximate the optimal bridge sampling estimator. Noting that

$$
\frac {q _ {3} ^ {\mathrm {o p t}} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta})} = \frac {q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{N _ {1} q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) + r N _ {2} q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}, \quad \frac {q _ {3} ^ {\mathrm {o p t}} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta})} = \frac {q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}{N _ {1} q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) + r N _ {2} q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta})}. \tag {74}
$$

The iterative procedure is formed by the following steps:

1. Start with an initial estimate  $\widehat{r}^{(1)}\approx \frac{c_1}{c_2}$  (using e.g. Laplace's).  
2. For  $t = 1,\dots,T$  ..

(a) Draw  $\{\pmb {\theta}_i\}_{i = 1}^{N_1}\sim \bar{q}_1(\pmb {\theta})$  and  $\{\mathbf{z}_i\}_{i = 1}^{N_2}\sim \bar{q}_2(\pmb {\theta})$  and iterate

$$
\widehat {r} ^ {(t + 1)} = \frac {\frac {1}{N _ {2}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \frac {q _ {1} \left(\mathbf {z} _ {i}\right)}{N _ {1} q _ {1} \left(\mathbf {z} _ {i}\right) + N _ {2} \widehat {r} ^ {(t)} q _ {2} \left(\mathbf {z} _ {i}\right)}}{\frac {1}{N _ {1}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \frac {q _ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}{N _ {1} q _ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right) + N _ {2} \widehat {r} ^ {(t)} q _ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}}. \tag {75}
$$

Remark 10. In [48, Theorem 3.3], the authors show that the asymptotic error of optimal bridge sampling with  $\bar{q}_3^{opt}$  in Eq. (73) is always greater than the asymptotic error of optimal umbrella sampling using  $\bar{q}_3^{opt}(\pmb{\theta}) \propto |\bar{q}_1(\pmb{\theta}) - \bar{q}_2(\pmb{\theta})|$  in Eq. (51).

Optimal bridge sampling for  $Z$ . Given the considerations above, an iterative bridge sampling

estimator of  $Z$  is obtained by setting  $q_{1}(\pmb{\theta}) = \pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ ,  $c_{1} = Z$ ,  $\bar{q}_{2}(\pmb{\theta}) = \bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$ , so that

$$
\widehat {Z} ^ {(t + 1)} = \frac {\frac {1}{N _ {2}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \frac {\pi (\mathbf {z} _ {i} | \mathbf {y})}{N _ {1} \pi (\mathbf {z} _ {i} | \mathbf {y}) + N _ {2} Z ^ {(t)} \bar {q} (\mathbf {z} _ {i})}}{\frac {1}{N _ {1}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \frac {\bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}{N _ {1} \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} | \mathbf {y}) + N _ {2} Z ^ {(t)} \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}}, \quad \left\{\mathbf {z} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {2}} \sim \bar {q} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \text {a n d} \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1}} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}). \tag {76}
$$

for  $t = 1, \dots, T$ . Looking at Eqs. (73) and (71), when  $N_{1} = 0$ , that is, when all samples are drawn from  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$ , the estimator above reduces to (non-iterative) standard IS scheme with proposal  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$ . When  $N_{2} = 0$ , that is, when all samples are drawn from  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ , the estimator becomes the (non-iterative) RIS estimator. See [48] for a comparison of optimal umbrella sampling, bridge sampling and path sampling (described in the next section). An alternative derivation of the optimal bridge sampling estimator is given in [46], by generating samples from a mixture of type  $\psi(\pmb{\theta}) \propto \pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}) + v\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$ . However, the resulting estimator employs the same samples drawn from  $\psi(\pmb{\theta})$  in the numerator and denominator, unlike in Eq. (76).

# 4.2.5 Other estimators drawing from a generic proposal and the posterior

Let consider again the scenario where we have a set of samples  $\{\pmb{\theta}_i\}_{i=1}^{N_1}$  from the posterior  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  and set  $\{\mathbf{z}_i\}_{i=1}^{N_2}$  from some proposal  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$ , as in the bridge sampling case described above. However, here we consider that these two sets  $\{\widetilde{\pmb{\theta}}_i\}_{i=1}^{N_1+N_2} = \{\{\pmb{\theta}_i\}_{i=1}^{N_1}, \{\mathbf{z}_i\}_{i=1}^{N_2}\}$  are drawn from the mixture  $\bar{q}_{\mathrm{mix}}(\pmb{\theta}) = \frac{N_1}{N_1 + N_2} P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}) + \frac{N_2}{N_1 + N_2} \bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  considering a deterministic mixture sampling approach [51], Thus, we can use the IS identities that use a single proposal, namely Eqs. (49) and (50).

Importance sampling with mixture (M-IS). Setting  $\bar{q}_1(\pmb{\theta}) = P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  and  $\bar{q}_2(\pmb{\theta}) = \bar{q}_{\mathrm{mix}}(\pmb{\theta})$  in Eq. (49), we have

$$
\widehat {Z} _ {\mathrm {M - I S}} = \frac {1}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \frac {\pi (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i} | \mathbf {y})}{\bar {q} _ {\operatorname {m i x}} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i})} = \frac {1}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \frac {\pi (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i} | \mathbf {y})}{\frac {N _ {1}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} P (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i} | \mathbf {y}) + \frac {N _ {2}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \bar {q} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i})}, \tag {77}
$$

where  $\widetilde{\pmb{\theta}}_i\sim \bar{q}_{\mathrm{mix}}(\pmb {\theta}) = \frac{N_1}{N_1 + N_2} P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y}) + \frac{N_2}{N_1 + N_2}\bar{q} (\pmb {\theta})$  [51]. This estimator cannot be directly used since it requires the evaluation of  $P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y}) = \frac{1}{Z}\pi (\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . From an initial guess  $\widetilde{Z}^{(0)}$ , the following iterative procedure can be used

$$
\widehat {Z} ^ {(t)} = \frac {1}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \frac {\widehat {Z} ^ {(t - 1)} \pi (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i} | \mathbf {y})}{\frac {N _ {1}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \pi (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i} | \mathbf {y}) + \frac {N _ {2}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \widehat {Z} ^ {(t - 1)} \bar {q} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i})}, \quad t \in \mathbb {N}. \tag {78}
$$

Self-IS with mixture proposal (M-Self-IS). Setting  $\bar{q}_1(\pmb{\theta}) = P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ ,  $\bar{q}_2(\pmb{\theta}) = \bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  and  $\bar{q}_3(\pmb{\theta}) = \bar{q}_{\mathrm{mix}}$  in Eq. (50), we have

$$
\widehat {Z} _ {\mathrm {M - S e l f - I S}} = \frac {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \frac {\pi (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i} | \mathbf {y})}{\bar {q} _ {\operatorname* {m i x}} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i})}}{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \frac {\bar {q} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i})}{\bar {q} _ {\operatorname* {m i x}} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i})}} = \frac {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \frac {\pi (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i} | \mathbf {y})}{\frac {N _ {1}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} P (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i}) + \frac {N _ {2}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \bar {q} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i})}}{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \frac {\bar {q} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i})}{\frac {N _ {1}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} P (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i} | \mathbf {y}) + \frac {N _ {2}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \bar {q} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i})}}, \tag {79}
$$

where  $\widetilde{\pmb{\theta}}_i\sim \bar{q}_{\mathrm{mix}} = \frac{N_1}{N_1 + N_2} P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y}) + \frac{N_2}{N_1 + N_2}\bar{q} (\pmb {\theta})$  (drawn in a deterministic way). As above, this estimator is not of direct use, so we need to iterate

$$
\widehat {Z} ^ {(t)} = \frac {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \frac {\pi (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i} | \mathbf {y})}{\frac {N _ {1}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \pi (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i}) + \frac {N _ {2}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \widehat {Z} ^ {(t - 1)} \bar {q} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i})}}{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \frac {\bar {q} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i})}{\frac {N _ {1}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \pi (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i} | \mathbf {y}) + \frac {N _ {2}}{N _ {1} + N _ {2}} \widehat {Z} ^ {(t - 1)} \bar {q} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {i})}}, \quad t \in \mathbb {N}. \tag {80}
$$

This iterative estimator is very similar to the iterative optimal bridge sampling estimator in Eq. (76), but it uses both set of samples in numerator and denominator. This estimator is also related to the reverse logistic regression method in [53] (for more details see [48, 54], and the next section). Furthermore, the iterative estimator (80) is also discussed for the case  $\bar{q} (\pmb {\theta}) = g(\pmb {\theta})$  in [55], in an attempt to exploit the advantages of the Naive Monte Carlo and the harmonic mean estimators, while removing their drawbacks.

Remark 11. Both iterative versions (78)-(80) converge to the optimal bridge sampling estimator (76). See [52], for a related discussion. As we show in the simulation study, the speed of convergence of each iterative method is different. The iterative bridge sampling estimator seems to be the quickest one.

# 4.2.6 Summary

Several techniques described in the last two subsections, including both umbrella and bridge sampling, are encompassed by the generic formula

$$
\frac {c _ {1}}{c _ {2}} = \mathbb {E} _ {\bar {\boldsymbol {\xi}}} [ q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \alpha (\boldsymbol {\theta}) ] / \mathbb {E} _ {\bar {\boldsymbol {\chi}}} [ q _ {2} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \alpha (\boldsymbol {\theta}) ] \tag {81}
$$

as shown in Table 8. The techniques differ also for which densities are drawn from and which densities are just evaluated.

# 4.3 IS based on multiple proposal densities

In this section we consider estimators of  $Z$  using samples drawn from more than two proposal densities. These schemes are usually based on the so-called tempering and/or annealing approach.

Reasons for tempering. The idea is again to consider densities that are in some sense "in the middle" between the posterior  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  and an easier-to-work-with density (e.g. the prior  $g(\pmb{\theta})$  or some other proposal density). These densities are usually scaled version of the posterior. Generally, the scale parameter is called temperature. For this reason, the resulting pdfs are usually named tempered posteriors and correspond to flatter, more diffuse distributions than the standard posterior. The use of the tempered pdfs usually improves the mixing of the MCMC algorithms and foster the exploration of the space  $\Theta$ . Generally, it helps the Monte Carlo methods (as MCMC and IS) to find the regions of posterior high probability. The number of such middle densities is

(59)</td><td>α(θ)</td><td>q2(θ)</td><td>q1(θ)</td><td rowspan="4">q1(θ)</td><td rowspan="4">q2(θ)</td><td rowspan="4">c1</td><td rowspan="4">c2</td><td>q1(θ), q2(θ)</td></tr><tr><td>Bridge Identity - Eq. (71)</td><td>q3(θ)/q2(θ)</td><td>q2(θ)</td><td>q1(θ)</td><td>q1(θ), q2(θ)</td></tr><tr><td>Identity - Eq. (49)</td><td>1/q2(θ)</td><td>q2(θ)</td><td>q1(θ)</td><td>q2(θ)</td></tr><tr><td>Umbrella - Eq. (50)</td><td>1/q3(θ)</td><td>q3(θ)</td><td>q3(θ)</td><td>q3(θ)</td></tr><tr><td colspan="9">For estimating Z, with one proposal</td></tr><tr><td>Self-norm. IS - Eq. (55)</td><td>1/q3(θ)</td><td>q3(θ)</td><td>q3(θ)</td><td>π(θ|y)</td><td>f(θ)</td><td rowspan="3">Z</td><td rowspan="3">1</td><td>q3(θ)</td></tr><tr><td>IS vers-1</td><td>1/q3(θ)</td><td>q3(θ)</td><td>P(θ|y)</td><td>π(θ|y)</td><td>q3(θ)</td><td>q3(θ)</td></tr><tr><td>RIS</td><td>1/π(θ|y)</td><td>q3(θ)</td><td>P(θ|y)</td><td>π(θ|y)</td><td>q3(θ)</td><td>P(θ|y)</td></tr><tr><td colspan="9">For estimating Z, with two proposals, P(θ|y) and q(θ)</td></tr><tr><td>Bridge Identity - Eq. (60)</td><td>α(θ)</td><td>q3(θ)</td><td>P(θ|y)</td><td>π(θ|y)</td><td>q3(θ)</td><td rowspan="3">Z</td><td rowspan="3">1</td><td rowspan="3">P(θ|y), q(θ)</td></tr><tr><td>Locally-Restricted IS</td><td>I3(θ)/q3(θ)</td><td>q3(θ)</td><td>P(θ|y)</td><td>π(θ|y)</td><td>q3(θ)</td></tr><tr><td>Locally-Restricted RIS</td><td>I3(θ)/q3(θ)</td><td>q3(θ)</td><td>P(θ|y)</td><td>π(θ|y)</td><td>q3(θ)</td></tr></table>

specified by the user, and in some cases, it is equivalent to the selection of a temperature schedule for linking the prior  $g(\pmb{\theta})$  and  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . This idea is shared by the several methods, such as path sampling, power posterior methods and stepping-stone sampling described below.

First of all, we start with a general IS scheme considering different proposals  $\bar{q}_n(\pmb{\theta})$ 's. Some of them could be tempered posteriors and the generation would be performed by an MCMC method in this case.

# 4.3.1 Multiple Importance Sampling (MIS) estimators

Here, we consider to generate samples from different proposal densities, i.e.,

$$
\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n} \sim \bar {q} _ {n} (\boldsymbol {\theta}), \quad n = 1, \dots , N. \tag {82}
$$

In this scenario, different proper importance weights can be used [51, 56, 57]. The most efficient MIS scheme considers the following weights

$$
w _ {n} = \frac {\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n} | \mathbf {y})}{\frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \bar {q} _ {i} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n})} = \frac {\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n} | \mathbf {y})}{\psi (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n})}, \tag {83}
$$

where  $\psi(\pmb{\theta}_n) = \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^{N} \bar{q}_i(\pmb{\theta}_n)$ . Indeed, considering the set of samples  $\{\pmb{\theta}_n\}_{n=1}^N$  drawn in a deterministic order,  $\pmb{\theta}_n \sim \bar{q}_n(\pmb{\theta})$ , and given a sample  $\pmb{\theta}^* \in \{\pmb{\theta}_1, \dots, \pmb{\theta}_N\}$  uniformly chosen in  $\{\pmb{\theta}_n\}_{n=1}^N$ ,

then we can write  $\pmb{\theta}^{*}\sim \psi (\pmb{\theta}_{n})$  . The standard MIS estimator is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widehat {Z} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} w _ {n} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\psi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n}\right)} (84) \\ = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} \frac {g \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n}\right) \ell \left(\mathbf {y} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n}\right)}{\psi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n}\right)}, (85) \\ = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} \eta_ {n} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n}), \quad \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n} \sim \bar {q} _ {n} (\boldsymbol {\theta}), \quad n = 1, \dots , N. (86) \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\eta_{n} = \frac{g(\pmb{\theta}_{n})}{\psi(\pmb{\theta}_{n})}$ . The estimator is unbiased [51]. As in the standard IS scheme, an alternative biased estimator is

$$
\widehat {Z} = \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} \bar {\eta} _ {n} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n}), \quad \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n} \sim \bar {q} _ {n} (\boldsymbol {\theta}), \quad n = 1, \dots , N, \tag {87}
$$

where  $\bar{\eta}_n = \frac{\eta_n}{\sum_{i=1}^N \eta_i}$ , so that  $\sum_{i=1}^N \bar{\eta}_i = 1$  and we have a convex combination of likelihood values  $\ell(\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}_n)$ 's. It is a generalization of the estimator in Eq. (40) and recalled below in Eq. (88).

# 4.3.2 Tempered posteriors as proposal densities

Let recall the IS vers-2 estimator of  $Z$  in Eq. (40), which involves a weighted sum of likelihood evaluations at points  $\{\pmb{\theta}_i\}_{i=1}^N$  drawn from importance density  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  (but we can evaluate only  $q(\pmb{\theta}) \propto \bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$ ),

$$
\widehat {Z} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \bar {\rho} _ {i} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}), \quad \bar {\rho} _ {i} = \frac {\frac {g \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}{q \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}}{\sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} \frac {g \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n}\right)}{q \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {n}\right)}} \propto \frac {g \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}{q \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right)}, \tag {88}
$$

where  $\sum_{i=1}^{N} \bar{\rho}_i = 1$ . Let us consider

$$
\bar {q} (\pmb {\theta}) = P (\pmb {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta) \propto q (\pmb {\theta}) = \pi (\pmb {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta) = g (\pmb {\theta}) \ell (\mathbf {y} | \pmb {\theta}) ^ {\beta},
$$

with  $\beta \in [0,1]$ . Namely, we use a tempered posterior as importance density. Note that we can evaluate only the unnormalized density  $q(\pmb{\theta})$ . The IS estimator version 2 can be employed in this case, and we obtain  $\bar{\rho}_i \propto \frac{g(\pmb{\theta}_i)}{g(\pmb{\theta}_i)\ell(\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}_i)^\beta} = \frac{1}{\ell(\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta}_i)^\beta}$ . The resulting IS estimator version 2 is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widehat {Z} = \frac {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {1}{\ell (\mathbf {y} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}) ^ {\beta}} \ell (\mathbf {y} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i})}{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {1}{\ell (\mathbf {y} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}) ^ {\beta}}} (89) \\ = \frac {\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}) ^ {1 - \beta}}{\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}) ^ {- \beta}} \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta) \quad \text {(v i a M C M C)}. (90) \\ \end{array}
$$

This method is denoted below as IS with a tempered posterior as proposal (IS-P). Table 9 shows that this technique includes different schemes for different values of  $\beta$ . Different possible MIS schemes can be also considered, i.e., using Eq. (87) for instance [51, 57].

Table 9: Different estimators of  $Z$  using  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta}) \propto g(\pmb{\theta})\ell(\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta})^\beta$  as importance density, with  $\beta \in [0,1]$ .  

<table><tr><td>Name</td><td>Coefficient β</td><td>Weights ρi</td><td>Estimator Z = ∑i=1Nρiℓ(y|θi)</td></tr><tr><td>Naive Monte Carlo</td><td>β = 0</td><td>1/N</td><td>1/N ∑i=1N ℓ(y|θi)</td></tr><tr><td>Harmonic Mean Estimator</td><td>β = 1</td><td>1/ℓ(y|θi)/∑j ℓ(y|θj)</td><td>Z = 1/1/N ∑i=1N ℓ(y|θi)</td></tr><tr><td>Power posterior as proposal pdf</td><td>0 &lt; β &lt; 1</td><td>1/ℓ(y|θi)/∑j ℓ(y|θj)</td><td>Z = ∑iℓ(y|θi)1-β/∑iℓ(y|θi)-β</td></tr></table>

Remark 12. One could consider also to draw samples from  $N$  different tempered posteriors,  $\pmb{\theta}_n\sim P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta_n)\propto g(\pmb {\theta})\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta})^{\beta_n}$ , with  $n = 1,\ldots ,N$ , and then apply deterministic mixture idea in (87). However, in this case, we cannot evaluate properly the mixture

$$
\psi (\pmb {\theta} _ {n}) = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} P (\pmb {\theta} _ {n} | \mathbf {y}, \beta_ {i}) = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {1}{Z (\beta_ {i})} \pi (\pmb {\theta} _ {n} | \mathbf {y}, \beta_ {i}).
$$

Here, the issue is a not just a global unknown normalizing constant (as usual): in this case, we do not know the weights of the mixture since all  $Z(\beta) = \int_{\Theta} g(\pmb{\theta}) \ell(\mathbf{y} | \pmb{\theta})^{\beta} d\pmb{\theta}$  are unknown. This problem can be solved using the techniques described in the next sections.

Reverse logistic regression (RLR). In RLR, the idea is to apply IS with the mixture  $\psi(\pmb{\theta}_n)$  in the remark above. The normalizing constants  $Z(\beta_i)$  are iteratively obtained by maximizing of a suitable log-likelihood, built with the samples from each tempered posterior  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta_n)$  [53, 58, 54].

In the next section, we describe an alternative to RLR for employing different tempered posteriors as proposals.

# 4.3.3 Stepping-stone (SS) sampling

Consider again  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta)\propto g(\pmb{\theta})\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta})^{\beta}$  and  $Z(\beta) = \int_{\Theta}g(\pmb {\theta})\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta})^{\beta}d\pmb{\theta}$ . The goal is to estimate  $Z = \frac{Z(1)}{Z(0)}$ , which can be expressed as the following product, with  $\beta_0 = 0$  and  $\beta_{K} = 1$

$$
Z = \frac {Z (1)}{Z (0)} = \prod_ {k = 1} ^ {K} \frac {Z \left(\beta_ {k}\right)}{Z \left(\beta_ {k - 1}\right)}, \tag {91}
$$

where  $\beta_{k}$  are often chosen as  $\beta_{k} = \frac{k}{K}$ ,  $k = 1, \ldots, K$ , i.e., with a uniform grid in  $[0,1]$ . Note that generally  $Z(0) = 1$ , since it is normalizing constant of the prior. The SS method is based on the following identity,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | y, \beta_ {k - 1})} \left[ \frac {\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k})}{\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k - 1})} \right] = \int_ {\Theta} \frac {\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k})}{\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k - 1})} P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | y, \beta_ {k - 1}) d \boldsymbol {\theta}, \\ = \frac {1}{Z (\beta_ {k - 1})} \int_ {\Theta} \pi (\pmb {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta_ {k}) d \pmb {\theta} = \frac {Z (\beta_ {k})}{Z (\beta_ {k - 1})}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Then, the idea of SS sampling is to estimate each ratio  $r_k = \frac{Z(\beta_k)}{Z(\beta_{k-1})}$  by importance sampling as

$$
r _ {k} = \frac {Z \left(\beta_ {k}\right)}{Z \left(\beta_ {k - 1}\right)} = \mathbb {E} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta_ {k - 1})} \left[ \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k}\right)}{\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k - 1}\right)} \right] \tag {92}
$$

$$
= \mathbb {E} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta_ {k - 1})} \left[ \frac {\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) ^ {\beta_ {k}}}{\ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) ^ {\beta_ {k - 1}}} \right] \tag {93}
$$

$$
\approx \widehat {r} _ {k} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i, k - 1}) ^ {\beta_ {k} - \beta_ {k - 1}}, \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i, k - 1} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta_ {k - 1}). \tag {94}
$$

Multiplying all ratio estimates yields the final estimator of  $Z$

$$
\widehat {Z} = \prod_ {k = 1} ^ {K} \widehat {r} _ {k} = \prod_ {k = 1} ^ {K} \left(\frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i, k - 1}) ^ {\beta_ {k} - \beta_ {k - 1}}\right), \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i, k - 1} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta_ {k - 1}). \tag {95}
$$

For  $K = 1$ , we come back to the Naive MC estimator. The sampling procedure of the SS method is graphically represented in Figure 2.

Remark 13. The SS estimator is unbiased, since it a product of unbiased estimators.

The two following methods, path sampling and power posteriors, estimate  $\log Z$  instead of  $Z$ .

![](images/318950e551b48a9dfd9622fdcd75777e29f326fa526863740f51fc87462ae2a9.jpg)  
Figure 2: Sampling procedure in the SS method. Note that samples from  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  ( $\beta_{K} = 1$ ) are not considered. It is relevant to compare this figure with Figures 4-5 in the next section.

# 4.3.4 Path sampling (a.k.a., thermodynamic integration)

More specifically, the method of path sampling for estimating  $\frac{c_1}{c_2}$  relies on the idea of building and drawing samples from a sequence of distributions linking  $\bar{q}_1(\pmb{\theta})$  and  $\bar{q}_2(\pmb{\theta})$  (a continuous path). For the purpose of estimating only one constant, the marginal likelihood  $Z$ , we set  $\bar{q}_2(\pmb{\theta}) = g(\pmb{\theta})$  and  $\bar{q}_1(\pmb{\theta}) = P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  and we link them by a univariate path with parameter  $\beta$ . Let

$$
\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta), \quad \beta \in [ 0, 1 ], \tag {96}
$$

denote a sequence of (probably unnormalized except for  $\beta = 0$ ) densities such  $\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta = 0) = g(\pmb{\theta})$  and  $\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta = 1) = \pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . More generally, we could consider  $\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta = 0) = \bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  where  $\bar{q}(\pmb{\theta})$  is a generic normalized proposal density, possibly closer to the posterior than  $g(\pmb{\theta})$ . The path sampling method for estimating the marginal likelihood is based on expressing  $\log Z$  as

$$
\log Z = \mathbb {E} _ {p (\boldsymbol {\theta}, \beta | \mathbf {y})} \left[ \frac {U (\boldsymbol {\theta} , \beta)}{p (\beta)} \right], \quad \text {w i t h} U (\boldsymbol {\theta}, \beta) = \frac {\partial}{\partial \beta} \log \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta), \tag {97}
$$

where the expectation is w.r.t. the joint  $p(\pmb{\theta}, \beta | \mathbf{y}) = \frac{1}{Z(\beta)} \pi(\pmb{\theta} | \mathbf{y}, \beta) p(\beta)$ , being  $Z(\beta)$  the normalizing constant of  $\pi(\pmb{\theta} | \mathbf{y}, \beta)$  and  $p(\beta)$  represents a density for  $\beta \in [0, 1]$ . Indeed, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {p (\pmb {\theta}, \beta | \mathbf {y})} \left[ \frac {U (\pmb {\theta} , \beta)}{p (\beta)} \right] = \int_ {\Theta} \int_ {0} ^ {1} \frac {1}{p (\beta)} \left[ \frac {\partial}{\partial \beta} \log \pi (\pmb {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta) \right] \frac {\pi (\pmb {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta)}{Z (\beta)} p (\beta) d \pmb {\theta} d \beta , \\ = \int_ {\Theta} \int_ {0} ^ {1} \frac {1}{\pi (\pmb {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta)} \left[ \frac {\partial}{\partial \beta} \pi (\pmb {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta) \right] \frac {\pi (\pmb {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta)}{Z (\beta)} d \pmb {\theta} d \beta , \\ = \int_ {\Theta} \int_ {0} ^ {1} \frac {1}{Z (\beta)} \frac {\partial}{\partial \beta} \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta) d \boldsymbol {\theta} d \beta , \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {1} \frac {1}{Z (\beta)} \frac {\partial}{\partial \beta} \left(\int_ {\Theta} \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta) d \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) d \beta , \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {1} \frac {1}{Z (\beta)} \frac {\partial}{\partial \beta} Z (\beta) d \beta \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {1} \frac {\partial}{\partial \beta} \log Z (\beta) d \beta = \log Z (1) - \log Z (0) = \log Z, \tag {98} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we substituted  $Z(\beta = 1) = Z(1) = Z$  and  $Z(\beta = 0) = Z(0) = 1$ . Thus, using a sample  $\{\pmb{\theta}_i,\beta_i\}_{i = 1}^N\sim p(\pmb {\theta},\beta |\mathbf{y})$ , we can write the path sampling estimator for  $\log Z$

$$
\widehat {\log Z} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \frac {U \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} , \beta_ {i}\right)}{p \left(\beta_ {i}\right)}, \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}, \beta_ {i} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim p (\boldsymbol {\theta}, \beta | \mathbf {y}). \tag {99}
$$

The samples from  $p(\pmb{\theta}, \beta | \mathbf{y})$  may be obtained by first drawing  $\beta'(\beta_i)$  from  $p(\beta)$  and then applying some MCMC steps to draw from  $P(\pmb{\theta} | \mathbf{y}, \beta') \propto \pi(\pmb{\theta} | \mathbf{y}, \beta')$  given  $\beta'$ . Therefore, in path sampling, we have to choose (a) the path and (b) and the prior  $p(\beta)$ . A discussion regarding the optimal choices of the path and  $p(\beta)$ , see [59]. The optimal path for linking any two given densities is impractical as it depends on the normalizing constants being estimated. The geometric path described below, although suboptimal, is generic and simple to implement.

Geometric path. Often a geometric path is employed,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta) = g (\boldsymbol {\theta}) ^ {1 - \beta} \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) ^ {\beta} \\ = g (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) ^ {\beta}, \quad \beta \in [ 0, 1 ]. \tag {100} \\ \end{array}
$$

Note that  $\pi (\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta)$  is the posterior with a powered, "less informative" - "wider" likelihood (for this reason,  $\pi (\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta)$  is often called a "power posterior"). In this case, we have

$$
U (\boldsymbol {\theta}, \beta) = \frac {\partial}{\partial \beta} \log \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta) = \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}),
$$

so the path sampling identity becomes

$$
\log Z = \mathbb {E} _ {p (\boldsymbol {\theta}, \beta | \mathbf {y})} \left[ \frac {\log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta})}{p (\beta)} \right], \tag {101}
$$

which is also used in the power posterior method of [60], described in Section 4.3.6.

# 4.3.5 Connections among path sampling, bridge sampling and stepping-stones

The path sampling method can be motivated from bridge sampling by applying the bridge sampling identity in (71) in a chain fashion. Assume we have  $K + 1$  densities  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta_k) = \pi (\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta_k) / Z(\beta_k)$ ,  $k = 0,\dots ,K$  from which we can draw samples, with endpoints  $P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta_0 = 0) = g(\pmb {\theta})$  and  $P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta_K = 1) = P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . We can express  $Z = Z(\beta_{K}) = Z(1)$  as follows

$$
Z = \prod_ {k = 1} ^ {K} \frac {Z \left(\beta_ {k}\right)}{Z \left(\beta_ {k - 1}\right)} = \prod_ {k = 1} ^ {K} \frac {\mathbb {E} _ {P \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k - 1}\right)} \left[ \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k - \frac {1}{2}}\right)}{\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k}\right)} \right]}{\mathbb {E} _ {P \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k}\right)} \left[ \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k - \frac {1}{2}}\right)}{\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} \mid \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k}\right)} \right]}. \tag {102}
$$

Note that we have applied the bridge sampling identity in Eq. (71) to each ratio  $\frac{Z(\beta_k)}{Z(\beta_{k-1})}$ , using  $K - 1$  middle densities  $\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta_{k-\frac{1}{2}})$ . We can approximate the  $k$ -th term by using samples from  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta_{k-1})$  and  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta_k)$ , and take the product to obtain the final estimator of  $Z$ . Taking the logarithm of the above expression, as  $K \to \infty$ , results in the basic identity of path sampling for estimating  $Z$  in Eq. (97) [59]. In this sense, path sampling can be interpreted as a continuous application of bridge sampling steps. The difference with SS method is that it employs another identity, in (92), for estimating the ratios  $\frac{Z(\beta_k)}{Z(\beta_{k-1})}$ . Figure 3 summarizes the relationships among the identities (49)-(71) and their multi-stages extensions: the SS method and path sampling scheme, respectively.

![](images/66a10f832085480d56434d2328887de15982b7b350ce2db98604d62d1a41b58f.jpg)  
Figure 3: Relationships among the identities (49)-(71) and their multi-stages extensions: the SS method and path sampling scheme, respectively.

# 4.3.6 Method of Power Posteriors

The previous expression (101) can also be converted into an integral in  $[0, 1]$  as follows

$$
\begin{array}{l} \log Z = \mathbb {E} _ {p (\pmb {\theta}, \beta | \mathbf {y})} \left[ \frac {\log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \pmb {\theta})}{p (\beta)} \right], \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {1} d \beta \int_ {\Theta} \frac {\log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta})}{p (\beta)} \frac {\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta)}{Z (\beta)} p (\beta) d \boldsymbol {\theta}, \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {1} d \beta \int_ {\Theta} \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) \frac {\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta)}{Z (\beta)} d \boldsymbol {\theta}, \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {1} \mathbb {E} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta)} [ \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) ] d \beta , \tag {103} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta) = \frac{\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta)}{Z(\beta)}$  is a power posterior. The power posterior method aims at estimating the integral above by applying a quadrature rule. For instance, choosing a discretization  $0 = \beta_0 < \beta_1 < \dots < \beta_{K - 1} < \beta_K = 1$ , leads to approximations of order 0,

$$
\widehat {\log Z} = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {K} \left(\beta_ {k} - \beta_ {k - 1}\right) \mathbb {E} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta_ {k - 1})} \left[ \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) \right], \tag {104}
$$

or order 1 (trapezoidal rule),

$$
\widehat {\log Z} = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {K} \left(\beta_ {k} - \beta_ {k - 1}\right) \frac {\mathbb {E} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k})} [ \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) ] + \mathbb {E} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k - 1})} [ \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) ]}{2}, \tag {105}
$$

where the expected values w.r.t. the power posteriors can be independently approximated via MCMC,

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta_ {k})} [ \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) ] \approx \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i, k}), \quad \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i, k} \right\} _ {i = 1} ^ {N} \sim P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta_ {k}), \quad k = 0, \dots , K. \tag {106}
$$

Remark 14. The identity (103) of method of power posteriors is derived by the path sampling identity with a geometric path, as shown in (100)-(101). In this sense, the method of power posteriors is a special case of path sampling. However, unlike in path sampling, the final approximation (105) is based on a deterministic quadrature.

Remark 15. Note that the approximation in Eq. (105) is biased due to using a deterministic quadrature, unlike the path sampling approximation in Eq. (99) which is unbiased.

Remark 16. The need of using several values  $\beta_{i}$  (i.e., several tempered posteriors) seems apparent in the estimator (99)-(105). For instance, in (105), the choice a small value of  $K$  yields a poor approximation of the integral (103). This is not the case in the SS method.

Extensions. Several improvements of the method of power posterior have been proposed in

the literature [61, 62]. In [61], the authors note that the derivative of the integrand in (103) corresponds to

$$
\frac {d}{d \beta} \mathbb {E} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta)} [ \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) ] = \operatorname {v a r} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta)} [ \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) ] \tag {107}
$$

so they propose to use this information to refine the trapezoidal rule in (105) by adding additional terms

$$
\widehat {\log Z} = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {K} \left(\beta_ {k} - \beta_ {k - 1}\right) \frac {\mathbb {E} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k})} \left[ \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) \right] + \mathbb {E} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y} , \beta_ {k - 1})} \left[ \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) \right]}{2} - \tag {108}
$$

$$
\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {K} \frac {\left(\beta_ {k} - \beta_ {k - 1}\right) ^ {2}}{1 2} \left[ \operatorname {v a r} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta_ {k})} [ \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) ] - \operatorname {v a r} _ {P (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}, \beta_ {k - 1})} [ \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) ] \right], \tag {109}
$$

This improvement comes at no extra cost since the same MCMC samples, used to estimate the expectations in (106), can be also used to estimate the variances in (109). They also propose constructing the temperature ladder recursively, starting from  $\beta_0 = 0$  and  $\beta_{K} = 1$ , by leveraging the estimates of  $\mathbb{E}_{P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta_k)}[\log \ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta})]$  and  $\mathrm{var}_{P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta_k)}[\log \ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta})]$  (for further details see [61, Sect. 2.2]). In [62], they propose the use of control variates, a variance reduction technique, in order to improve the statistical efficiency of the estimator (105). However, this can only be applied in settings where  $\nabla_{\pmb{\theta}}\log P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta)$  is available.

# 4.3.7 On the selection of  $\beta_{k}$

The method of power posteriors and SS sampling require setting an increasing sequence of  $\beta$ 's. Some strategies for selecting the sequence of values  $\beta_{k}$ 's, with  $\beta_0 = 0$  and  $\beta_{K} = 1$ , are discussed, e.g., in [60, 61, 63]. A uniform sequence  $\beta_{k} = \frac{k}{K}$  for  $k = 0,\dots ,K$  can be considered, although [60] recommends putting more values near  $\beta = 0$ , since it is where  $P(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y},\beta)$  is changing more rapidly. More generally, we can consider  $\beta_{k} = (\frac{k}{K})^{1 / \alpha}$ . For choice of  $\alpha \in [0,1]$ , the values  $\beta_{k}$  are evenly-spaced quantiles of a  $\mathrm{Beta}(\alpha ,1)$ , concentrating more and more near  $\beta = 0$  as  $\alpha$  decreases to 0 [63].

The path sampling method requires defining a prior density  $p(\beta)$  from which samples are drawn. It can be shown that, for any given path, the optimal choice of  $p(\beta)$  is a generalized local Jeffreys prior [59, Sect. 4.1].

# 4.3.8 Connection between stepping-stone and power posteriors methods

Taking the logarithm of the SS estimator (95), we obtain

$$
\log \widehat {Z} _ {\mathrm {S S}} = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {K} \log \left(\frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \ell (\mathbf {y} | \pmb {\theta} _ {i, k - 1}) ^ {\beta_ {k} - \beta_ {k - 1}}\right).
$$

Applying the Jensen inequality and property of the logarithm, we can write

$$
\begin{array}{l} \log \widehat {Z} _ {\mathrm {S S}} \geq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {K} \left(\frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i, k - 1}) ^ {\beta_ {k} - \beta_ {k - 1}}\right), \\ \geq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {K} (\beta_ {k} - \beta_ {k - 1}) \left(\frac {1}{N} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {N} \log \ell (\mathbf {y} | \pmb {\theta} _ {i, k - 1})\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

The last expression is the estimator of the power posteriors method of order 0, i.e., replacing Eq. (106) into (104). If we denote here this estimator here as  $\widehat{\log Z}_{PP}$ , then we have  $\log \widehat{Z}_{\mathrm{SS}} \geq \widehat{\log Z}_{PP}$ . Recall also the SS estimator is unbiased.

# 5 Advanced schemes combining MCMC and IS

In the previous sections, we have already introduced several methods which require the use of MCMC algorithms in order to draw from complex proposal densities. The RIS estimator, path sampling, power posteriors and the SS sampling schemes are some examples. All these previous schemes could be assigned to the family of "MCMC-within-IS" techniques. In this section, we describe more sophisticated schemes for estimating the evidence, which combine MCMC and IS techniques: Annealed Importance Sampling (An-IS) in Section 5.1, Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) in Section 5.2, Multiple Try Metropolis (MTM) in Section 5.3, and Layered Adaptive importance Sampling (LAIS) in Section 5.4. An-IS and SMC can be also considered "MCMC-within-IS" techniques. They provide alternative ways to employ tempered posteriors and are related to SS method, described in the previous section. We also discuss the use of MCMC transitions and resampling steps for design efficient AIS schemes. The MTM algorithm described here is an MCMC method, which belongs to the family of "IS-within-MCMC" techniques. Indeed, internal IS steps are used for proposing good candidates as new state of the chain. LAIS is an AIS scheme driven by MCMC transitions. Since the adaptation and sampling parts can be completely separated, LAIS can be considered as a "IS-after-MCMC" technique.

# 5.1 MCMC-within-IS: weighted samples after MCMC iterations

In this section, we will see how to properly weight samples obtained by different MCMC iterations. We denote as  $K(\mathbf{z}|\boldsymbol{\theta})$  the transition kernel which summarizes all the steps of the employed MCMC algorithm. Note that generally  $K(\mathbf{z}|\boldsymbol{\theta})$  cannot be evaluated. However, we can use MCMC kernels  $K(\mathbf{z}|\boldsymbol{\theta})$  in the same fashion as proposal densities, considering the concept of the so-called proper weighting [1, 64].

# 5.1.1 Weighting a sample after one MCMC iteration

Let us consider the following procedure:

1. Draw  $\pmb{\theta}_0 \sim q(\pmb{\theta})$  (where  $q(\pmb{\theta})$  is normalized, for simplicity).

2. Draw  $\pmb{\theta}_{1} \sim K(\pmb{\theta}_{1}|\pmb{\theta}_{0})$ , where the kernel  $K$  leaves invariant density  $\bar{\eta}(\pmb{\theta}) = \frac{1}{c}\eta(\pmb{\theta})$ , i.e.,

$$
\int_ {\Theta} K (\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\prime} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) \bar {\eta} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} = \bar {\eta} (\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {\prime}). \tag {110}
$$

3. Assign to  $\pmb{\theta}_{1}$  the weight

$$
\rho \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}\right) = \frac {\eta \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0}\right)}{q \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0}\right)} \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\eta \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}\right)}. \tag {111}
$$

This weight is proper in the sense that can be used for building unbiased estimator  $Z$  (or other moments  $P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ ), as described in the Liu's definition [2, Section 14.2], [1, Section 2.5.4]. Indeed, we can write

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} [ \rho (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}) ] = \int_ {\Theta} \int_ {\Theta} \rho (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}) K (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0}) q (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} d \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}, \\ { = } { \int _ { \Theta } \int _ { \Theta } \frac { \eta ( \pmb { \theta } _ { 0 } ) } { q ( \pmb { \theta } _ { 0 } ) } \frac { \pi ( \pmb { \theta } _ { 1 } ) } { \eta ( \pmb { \theta } _ { 1 } ) } K ( \pmb { \theta } _ { 1 } | \pmb { \theta } _ { 0 } ) q ( \pmb { \theta } _ { 0 } ) d \pmb { \theta } _ { 0 } d \pmb { \theta } _ { 1 } , } \\ = \int_ {\Theta} \frac {\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1})}{\eta (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1})} \left[ \int_ {\Theta} \eta (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0}) K (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} \right] d \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}, \\ = \int_ {\Theta} \frac {\pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1})}{c \bar {\eta} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1})} c \bar {\eta} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} = \int_ {\Theta} \pi (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} | \mathbf {y}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} = Z. \tag {112} \\ \end{array}
$$

Note that if  $\eta(\pmb{\theta}) \equiv \pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  then  $\rho(\pmb{\theta}_1) = \frac{\pi(\pmb{\theta}_0|\mathbf{y})}{q(\pmb{\theta}_0)}$ , i.e., the IS weights remain unchanged after an MCMC iteration with invariant density  $\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . Hence, if we repeat the procedure above  $N$  times generating  $\{\pmb{\theta}_0^{(n)}, \pmb{\theta}_1^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^N$ , we can build the following unbiased estimator of the  $Z$ ,

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} \rho \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} ^ {(n)}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} ^ {(n)}\right) = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} \frac {\eta \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} ^ {(n)}\right)}{q \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} ^ {(n)}\right)} \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} ^ {(n)} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\eta \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} ^ {(n)}\right)} \tag {113}
$$

In the next section, we extend this idea where different MCMC updates are applied, each one addressing a different invariant density.

# 5.1.2 Annealed Importance Sampling (An-IS)

In the previous section, we have considered the application of one MCMC kernel  $K(\pmb{\theta}_1|\pmb{\theta}_0)$  (that could be formed by different MCMC steps). Below, we consider the application of several MCMC kernels addressing different target pdfs, and show their consequence in the weighting strategy. We consider again a sequence of tempered versions of the posterior,  $\pi_1(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}), \pi_2(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}), \dots, \pi_L(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}) \equiv \pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ , where the  $L$ -th version,  $\pi_L(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ , coincides with the target function  $\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . One possibility is to consider  $\pi_i(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}) = [\pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})]^{\beta_i} = g(\pmb{\theta})^{\beta_i}\ell(\mathbf{y}|\pmb{\theta})^{\beta_i}$  or tempered posteriors,

$$
\pi_ {i} (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) = g (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \ell (\mathbf {y} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) ^ {\beta_ {i}} \quad \text {w h e r e} \quad 0 \leq \beta_ {1} \leq \beta_ {2} \leq \dots \leq \beta_ {L} = 1. \tag {114}
$$

as in path sampling and power posteriors. In any case, smaller  $\beta$  values correspond to flatter distributions. $^4$  The use of the tempered sequence of target pdfs usually improves the mixing of the algorithm and foster the exploration of the space  $\Theta$ . Since only the last function is the true target,  $\pi_L(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}) = \pi (\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ , different schemes have been proposed for suitable weighting the final samples.

Let us consider conditional  $L - 1$  kernels  $K_{i}(\mathbf{z}|\pmb{\theta})$  (with  $L \geq 2$ ), representing the probability of different MCMC updates of jumping from the state  $\pmb{\theta}$  to the state  $\mathbf{z}$  (note that each  $K_{i}$  can summarize the application of several MCMC steps), each one leaving invariant a different tempered target,  $P_{i}(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}) \propto \pi_{i}(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . The Annealed Importance Sampling (An-IS) is given in Table 10. Note

# Table 10: Annealed Importance Sampling (An-IS)

1. Draw  $N$  samples  $\pmb{\theta}_0^{(n)}\sim P_0(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  (usually  $g(\pmb {\theta})$ ) for  $n = 1,\ldots ,N$  
2. For  $k = 1, \dots, L - 1$ :

(a) Draw  $\pmb{\theta}_{k}^{(n)} \sim K_{k}(\pmb{\theta}|\pmb{\theta}_{k-1}^{(n)})$  leaving invariant  $P_{k}(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  for  $n = 1, \dots, N$ , i.e., we generate  $N$  samples using an MCMC with invariant distribution  $P_{k}(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  (with different starting points  $\pmb{\theta}_{k-1}^{(n)}$ ).  
(b) Compute the weight associated to the sample  $\pmb{\theta}_k^{(n)}$ , for  $n = 1, \dots, N$ ,

$$
\rho_ {k} ^ {(n)} = \prod_ {i = 0} ^ {k} \frac {\pi_ {i + 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} ^ {(n)} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\pi_ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} ^ {(n)} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)} = \rho_ {k - 1} ^ {(n)} \frac {\pi_ {k + 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} ^ {(n)} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\pi_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} ^ {(n)} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}. \tag {115}
$$

3. Return the weighted sample  $\{\pmb{\theta}_{L-1}^{(n)}, \rho_{L-1}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^N$ . The estimator of the marginal likelihood is

$$
\widehat {Z} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} \rho_ {L - 1} ^ {(n)}.
$$

Combinations of An-IS with path sampling and power posterior methods can be also considered, employing the information of the rest of intermediate densities.

that, when  $L = 2$ , we have  $\rho_1^{(n)} = \frac{\pi_1(\pmb{\theta}_0^{(n)}|\mathbf{y})}{q(\pmb{\theta}_0^{(n)})} \frac{\pi(\pmb{\theta}_1^{(n)}|\mathbf{y})}{\pi_1(\pmb{\theta}_1^{(n)}|\mathbf{y})}$ . If,  $\pi_1 = \pi_2 = \ldots = \pi_{L-1} = \eta \neq \pi$ , then the weight is  $\rho_{L-1} = \frac{\eta(\pmb{\theta}_0^{(n)})}{P_0(\pmb{\theta}_0^{(n)}|\mathbf{y})} \frac{\pi(\pmb{\theta}_{L-1}^{(n)}|\mathbf{y})}{\eta(\pmb{\theta}_{L-1}^{(n)})}$ .

The method above can be modified by incorporating an additional MCMC transition  $\pmb{\theta}_{L} \sim K_{L}(\pmb{\theta}|\pmb{\theta}_{L-1})$ , which leaves invariant  $P_{L}(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}) = P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ . However, since  $P_{L}(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  is the true target pdf, as we have seen above the weight remains unchanged (see the case  $\bar{\eta}(\pmb{\theta}) = P(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  in the previous section). Hence, in this scenario, the output would be  $\{\pmb{\theta}_{L}^{(n)}, \rho_{L}^{(n)}\} = \{\pmb{\theta}_{L}^{(n)}, \rho_{L-1}^{(n)}\}$ , i.e.,

$\rho_{L}^{(n)} = \rho_{L - 1}^{(n)}$ . This method has been proposed in [66] but similarly schemes can be found in [65, 67].

Remark 17. The stepping-stones (SS) sampling method described in Section 4.3 is strictly connected to an Ann-IS scheme. See Figures 2 and 4 for a comparison of the sampling procedures.

Interpretation as Standard IS. For the sake of simplicity, here we consider reversible kernels, i.e., each kernel satisfies the detailed balance condition

$$
\pi_ {i} (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y}) K _ {i} (\mathbf {z} | \boldsymbol {\theta}) = \pi_ {i} (\mathbf {z} | \mathbf {y}) K _ {i} (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {z}) \quad \text {s o t h a t} \quad \frac {K _ {i} (\mathbf {z} | \boldsymbol {\theta})}{K _ {i} (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {z})} = \frac {\pi_ {i} (\mathbf {z} | \mathbf {y})}{\pi_ {i} (\boldsymbol {\theta} | \mathbf {y})}. \tag {116}
$$

We show that the weighting strategy suggested by An-IS can be interpreted as a standard IS weighting considering the following extended target density, defined in the extended space  $\Theta^L$ ,

$$
\pi_ {g} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}, \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {L - 1} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) = \pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {L - 1} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) \prod_ {k = 1} ^ {L - 1} K _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k}\right). \tag {117}
$$

Note that  $\pi_g$  has the true target  $\pi$  as a marginal pdf. Let also consider an extended proposal pdf defined as

$$
q _ {g} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}, \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {L - 1}\right) = P _ {0} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) \prod_ {k = 1} ^ {L - 1} K _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1}\right). \tag {118}
$$

The standard IS weight of an extended sample  $[\pmb{\theta}_0, \pmb{\theta}_1, \dots, \pmb{\theta}_{L-1}]$  in the extended space  $\Theta^L$  is

$$
w \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}, \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {L - 1}\right) = \frac {\pi_ {g} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} , \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {L - 1} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{q _ {g} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} , \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {L - 1}\right)} = \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {L - 1} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) \prod_ {k = 1} ^ {L - 1} K _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k}\right)}{P _ {0} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) \prod_ {k = 1} ^ {L - 1} K _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1}\right)}. \tag {119}
$$

Replacing the expression  $\frac{K_i(\mathbf{z}|\boldsymbol{\theta})}{K_i(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathbf{z})} = \frac{\pi_i(\mathbf{z}|\mathbf{y})}{\pi_i(\boldsymbol{\theta}|\mathbf{y})}$  in (119), we obtain the Ann-IS weights

$$
\begin{array}{l} w \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}, \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {L - 1}\right) = \frac {\pi \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {L - 1} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{P _ {0} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)} \prod_ {k = 1} ^ {L - 1} \frac {\pi_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\pi_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}, (120) \\ = \frac {\pi_ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{P _ {0} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {0} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)} \prod_ {k = 1} ^ {L - 1} \frac {\pi_ {k + 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\pi_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)} = \prod_ {k = 0} ^ {L - 1} \frac {\pi_ {k + 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\pi_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)} = \rho_ {L - 1}, (121) \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have used  $\pi_L(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y}) = \pi(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  and just rearranged the numerator. The sampling procedure in An-IS is graphically represented in Figure 4.

# 5.2 Weighted samples after MCMC and resampling steps

In this section, we consider also the use of resampling steps jointly with MCMC transitions. The resulting algorithm is quite sophisticated (formed by several components that should be chosen by the user) but it is a very general technique, which includes the classical particle filters, several adaptive IS (AIS) schemes and the An-IS method as special case [68].

![](images/a0898adb13b7d7d4ae6b2dac5cc037d116f393c6e2677db26c506a42246fbd71.jpg)  
Figure 4: Sampling procedure in the An-IS method.

# 5.2.1 Generic Sequential Monte Carlo

In this section, we describe a sequential IS scheme which encompasses the previous Ann-IS algorithm as a special case. The method described here uses jointly MCMC transitions and, additionally, resampling steps as well. It is called Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC), since we have a sequence of target pdfs  $\pi_k(\pmb{\theta}|\mathbf{y})$ ,  $k = 1,\dots ,L$  [68]. This sequence of target densities can be defined by a state-space model as in a classical particle filtering framework (truly sequential scenario, where the goal is to track dynamic parameters). Alternatively, we can also consider a static scenario as in the previous sections, i.e., the resulting algorithm is an iterative importance sampler where we consider a sequence of tempered densities  $\pi_k(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y}) = g(\pmb {\theta})\ell (\mathbf{y}|\pmb {\theta})^{\beta_k}$ , where  $0\leq \beta_{1}\leq \ldots \leq \beta_{L} = 1$ , as in Eq.(114), so that  $\pi_L(\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y}) = \pi (\pmb {\theta}|\mathbf{y})$  [68]. Let us again define an extended proposal density in the domain  $\Theta^k$ ,

$$
\widetilde {q} _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}, \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k}\right) = q _ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}\right) \prod_ {i = 2} ^ {k} F _ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i - 1}\right): \quad \Theta^ {k} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}, \tag {122}
$$

where  $q_{1}(\pmb{\theta}_{1})$  is a marginal proposal and  $F_{i}(\pmb{\theta}_{i}|\pmb{\theta}_{i-1})$  are generic forward transition pdfs, that will be used as partial proposal pdfs. Extending the space from  $\Theta^{k}$  to  $\Theta^{k+1}$  (increasing its dimension), note that we can write the recursive equation

$$
\widetilde {q} _ {k + 1} (\pmb {\theta} _ {1}, \dots , \pmb {\theta} _ {k}, \pmb {\theta} _ {k + 1}) = F _ {k + 1} (\pmb {\theta} _ {k + 1} | \pmb {\theta} _ {k}) \widetilde {q} _ {k} (\pmb {\theta} _ {1}, \dots , \pmb {\theta} _ {k}): \Theta^ {k + 1} \to \mathbb {R}.
$$

The marginal proposal pdfs are

$$
\begin{array}{l} q _ {k} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k}) = \int_ {\Theta^ {k - 1}} \widetilde {q} _ {k} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}, \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1: k - 1} \\ = \int_ {\Theta^ {k - 1}} q _ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}\right) \prod_ {i = 2} ^ {k} F _ {i} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i - 1}\right) d \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1: k - 1}, (123) \\ = \int_ {\Theta} \left[ \int_ {\Theta^ {k - 2}} q _ {1} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}) \prod_ {i = 2} ^ {k} F _ {i} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i - 1}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1: k - 2} \right] F _ {k} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1}) d \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1}, \\ = \int_ {\Theta} q _ {k - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1}\right) F _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1}\right) d \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1}, (124) \\ \end{array}
$$

Therefore, we would be interested in computing the marginal IS weights,  $w_{k} = \frac{\pi_{k}(\pmb{\theta}_{k}|\mathbf{y})}{q_{k}(\pmb{\theta}_{k})}$ , for each  $k$ . However note that, in general, the marginal proposal pdfs  $q_{k}(\pmb{\theta}_{k})$  cannot be computed and then cannot be evaluated. A suitable alternative approach is described next. Let us consider the extended target pdf defined as

$$
\widetilde {\pi} _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}, \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) = \pi_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) \prod_ {i = 2} ^ {k} B _ {i - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i - 1} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}\right): \quad \Theta^ {k} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}, \tag {125}
$$

$B_{i - 1}(\pmb{\theta}_{i - 1}|\pmb{\theta}_i)$  are arbitrary backward transition pdfs. Note that the space of  $\{\widetilde{\pi}_k\}$  increases as  $k$  grows, and  $\pi_k$  is always a marginal pdf of  $\widetilde{\pi}_k$ . Moreover, writing the previous equation for  $k + 1$

$$
\widetilde {\pi} _ {k + 1} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1}, \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k}, \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k + 1} | \mathbf {y}) = \pi_ {k + 1} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k + 1} | \mathbf {y}) \prod_ {i = 2} ^ {k + 1} B _ {i - 1} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i - 1} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {i}),
$$

and writing the ratio of both, we get

$$
\frac {\widetilde {\pi} _ {k + 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} , \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k + 1} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\widetilde {\pi} _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} , \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)} = \frac {\pi_ {k + 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k + 1} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\pi_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)} B _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k + 1}\right). \tag {126}
$$

Therefore, the IS weights in the extended space  $\Theta^k$  are

$$
\begin{array}{l} w _ {k} = \frac {\widetilde {\pi} _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} , \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\widetilde {q} _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} , \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k}\right)} (127) \\ = \frac {\widetilde {\pi} _ {k - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} , \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\widetilde {q} _ {k - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {1} , \dots , \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1}\right)} \frac {\frac {\pi_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\pi_ {k - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)} B _ {k - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k}\right)}{F _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1}\right)}, (128) \\ = w _ {k - 1} \frac {\pi_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) B _ {k - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k}\right)}{\pi_ {k - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) F _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1}\right)}. (129) \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have replaced  $w_{k - 1} = \frac{\widetilde{\pi}_{k - 1}(\pmb{\theta}_{1},\dots,\pmb{\theta}_{k - 1}|\mathbf{y})}{\widetilde{q}_{k - 1}(\pmb{\theta}_{1},\dots,\pmb{\theta}_{k - 1})}$ . The recursive formula in Eq. (129) is the key expression for several sequential IS techniques. The SMC scheme summarized in Table 11 is a general framework which contains different algorithms as a special cases [68]. In Table 11, we have used the notation  $\pmb{\theta}_{1:k} = [\pmb{\theta}_1,\dots,\pmb{\theta}_k]$ .

Table 11: Generic Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC)

1. Draw  $\pmb{\theta}_{1}^{(n)}\sim q_{1}(\pmb {\theta})$ $n = 1,\dots ,N$  
2. For  $k = 2, \dots, L$ :

(a) Draw  $N$  samples  $\pmb{\theta}_{k}^{(n)}\sim F_{k}(\pmb {\theta}|\pmb{\theta}_{k - 1}^{(n)}).$  
(b) Compute the weights

$$
\begin{array}{l} w _ {k} ^ {(n)} = w _ {k - 1} ^ {(n)} \frac {\pi_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} ^ {(n)} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) B _ {k - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} ^ {(n)} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} ^ {(n)}\right)}{\pi_ {k - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} ^ {(n)} \mid \mathbf {y}\right) F _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} ^ {(n)}\right)}, (130) \\ = w _ {k - 1} ^ {(n)} \gamma_ {k} ^ {(n)}, \quad , k = 1, \dots , L, (131) \\ \end{array}
$$

where we set  $\gamma_k^{(n)} = \frac{\pi_k(\pmb{\theta}_k^{(n)}|\mathbf{y})B_{k-1}(\pmb{\theta}_{k-1}^{(n)}|\pmb{\theta}_k^{(n)})}{\pi_{k-1}(\pmb{\theta}_{k-1}^{(n)}|\mathbf{y})F_k(\pmb{\theta}_k|\pmb{\theta}_{k-1}^{(n)})}$ .

(c)Normalize the weights  $\bar{w}_k^{(n)} = \frac{w_k^{(n)}}{\sum_{j = 1}^Nw_k^{(j)}}$  ,for  $n = 1,\ldots ,N$  
(d) If  $\widehat{ESS} \leq \epsilon N$ : (with  $0 \leq \epsilon \leq 1$  and  $\widehat{ESS}$  is a effective sample size measure [69], see section 5.2.2)

i. Resample  $N$  times  $\{\pmb{\theta}_{1:k}^{(1)},\dots,\pmb{\theta}_{1:k}^{(N)}\}$  according to  $\{\bar{w}_k^{(n)}\}_{n = 1}^N$ , obtaining  $\{\bar{\pmb{\theta}}_{1:k}^{(1)},\dots,\bar{\pmb{\theta}}_{1:k}^{(N)}\}$ .  
ii. Set  $\pmb{\theta}_{1:k}^{(n)} = \bar{\pmb{\theta}}_{1:k}^{(n)}$ ,  $\widehat{Z}_k = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{n = 1}^N w_k^{(n)}$  and  $w_{k}^{(n)} = \widehat{Z}_{k}$  for all  $n = 1,\ldots ,N$  [70, 64, 71, 20].

3. Return the cloud of weighted particles and

$$
\widehat {Z} = \widehat {Z} _ {L} = \frac {1}{N} \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {N} w _ {L} ^ {(n)},
$$

if a proper weighting of the resampled particles is used (as suggested in the step 2(d)-ii above). Otherwise, you can use another estimator  $\widehat{Z}_L$ , as shown in Section 5.2.2 and the Supplementary Material.

Choice of the forward functions. One possible choice is to use independent proposal pdfs, i.e.,  $F_{k}(\pmb{\theta}_{k}|\pmb{\theta}_{k-1}) = F_{k}(\pmb{\theta}_{k})$  or random walk proposal  $F_{k}(\pmb{\theta}_{k}|\pmb{\theta}_{k-1})$ , where  $F_{k}$  represents standard distributions (e.g., Gaussian or t-Student). An alternative is to choose  $F_{k}(\pmb{\theta}_{k}|\pmb{\theta}_{k-1}) = K_{k}(\pmb{\theta}_{k}|\pmb{\theta}_{k-1})$ , i.e., an MCMC kernel with invariant pdf  $P_{k}(\pmb{\theta}_{k}|\mathbf{y})$ .

Choice of backward functions. It is possible to show that the optimal backward transitions  $\{B_k\}_{k=1}^L$  are [68]

$$
B _ {k - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k}\right) = \frac {q _ {k - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1}\right)}{q _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k}\right)} F _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1}\right). \tag {132}
$$

This choice reduces the variance of the weights [68]. However, generally, the marginal proposal  $q_{k}$  in Eq. (123) cannot be computed (are not available), other possible  $\{B_k\}$  should be considered. For instance, with the choice

$$
B _ {k - 1} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k}\right) = \frac {\pi_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)}{\pi_ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \mathbf {y}\right)} F _ {k} \left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} \mid \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1}\right), \tag {133}
$$

we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} w _ {k} = w _ {k - 1} \frac {\pi_ {k} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} | \mathbf {y}) \frac {\pi_ {k} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} | \mathbf {y})}{\pi_ {k} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} | \mathbf {y})} F _ {k} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1})}{\pi_ {k - 1} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} | \mathbf {y}) F _ {k} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k} | \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1})} (134) \\ = w _ {k - 1} \frac {\pi_ {k} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} | \mathbf {y})}{\pi_ {k - 1} (\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {k - 1} | \mathbf {y})}, (135) \\ \end{array}
$$

which is exactly the update rule for the weights in An-IS.

Remark 18. With the choice of  $B_{k - 1}(\pmb{\theta}_{k - 1}|\pmb{\theta}_k)$  as in Eq. 133, and if  $F_{k}(\pmb{\theta}_{k}|\pmb{\theta}_{k - 1}) = K_{k}(\pmb{\theta}_{k}|\pmb{\theta}_{k - 1})$  is an MCMC kernel with invariant  $P_{k}(\pmb{\theta}_{k}|\mathbf{y})$ , then we come back to An-IS algorithm [66, 65, 67], described in Table 10. Hence, the An-IS scheme is a special case of SMC method.
